VERY STRICT.—In Germany the police regula- tions are very strict, and any violation of them is promptly punished. The people have an inherent terror of the law. Two gentlemen happened to meet in Berlin, and the following conversation, it is alleged, took place :—" Have you heard the dreadful news about Muller ? " "No ; what is it?" "He was in a boat on the river. He fell overboard and was drowned. The water was too deep." "Didn't he know how to swim?" "Swim! Don't you know that all persons are for- bidden by the police to swim in the river?" THE BILL-STICKER'S POLITICS.—A rural bill- sticker, who had to do duty for both candidates, was asked which party he preferred, when he said: "When I starts on my rounds, I thinks, William 'Arcourt—'n's the man for me! None of your bloomin' lords, but just a plain man like myself!' So I sticks up his posters. But when I starts on my second rounds, I thinks as how I've been unfair to my noble Lord of Salisbury, and the confidence 'e reposes in me in gending me 'is bills; so I sticks them right over 'Arcourt's, so that both parties have their turn, and their confidence in inc is justified." WHY T I LEY READ UPSIDE DOWN. —Mr. and Mrs. Wings were reading the evening paper. "Peculiar ways the Japanese have," said Mrs. Wings, casually : " they read up instead of down, and from right to left. What do you suppose they do that for, Mr. Wings?" "How should I know, Mrs. Wings? What questions you women do ask." 'Then there was silence, until suddenly Wings threw down the paper with a remark of an electric-blue colour and a milestone smell and began poking the fire viciously. "What is the matter, Mr. Wings?" eagerly asked his wife. " Matter ? Oh, nothing—nothing at all. I only read a half-column of thrilling narrative before I fond that it wound up with a patent medicine -advertis,2nnt." Again there was silence. Then Mrs. Wings, who had been pondering something deeply, sad: "I know now why the Japanese read up instead Of down." Oh, you do, eh? Well, why?" "So ;Ls to see the patent, medicine advertisement before reading the article. '

Of all the efforts made in recent years to revive old industries in various parts of Ireland none have succeeded better than those which have been directed to the production of coloured linens. A varied selection of these fabrics on Monday on view at Donegal House, Wigmore-st., revealed the very high degree of perfectioh which has been at- tained. No fewer than 500 combinations of tints are manufactured, the splendid Oriental reds and blues being quite as durable as their Egyptian pro- totypes oE 2,000 years ago. There is a distinct in- dividuality about these linens, all of them made on hand-looms in cottages, and the softer-tinted kinds are fpecially adapted for decorative purposes. A few specimens stencilled in silver and gold with original Japanese designs and after Japanese methods instanced the fine effects arrived at by , simple means and at a comparatively low cost. A I better background for pictures can hardly be imagined. Rich Eastern fabrics are a luxury, but these Irish linens, well within reach of the mode- rately filled purse, are in their way quite as beau- tiful, and certainly not less truly artistic. Certain finer kinds woven for dress use are " shot " in a manner altogether novel and attractive. Thanks to the example of many leaders of fashion,the linen industry bids fair to answer the expectations of the ladies wllo have worked so energetically for its restoration.

The directors of the Chartered Company, what- ever they may do from motives of policy, are, it is said, equally divi led in numbers as to the aban- donment of Mr. Cecil Ehodes Mr. Rochefort Maguire, ex-Parnellite M.P., has not been slow to point to the disorganisation which followed the abandonment of Parnell on an issue of morals. What will be attempted, therefore, will, we are as- sured, be a compromise—the retirement of Mr. Rhodes for a season, and his reinstatement as soon as time may be supposed to have deadened the prickings of the public conscience or the situation in South Africa has definitively cleared up.

Although Dr. Jameson has given a denial to the rumours about his approaching marriage,those who profess to know something about the matter take the denial as simply implying that the marriage is not immediately imminent. According to the story current in some circles, the attachment is of long standing. The lady, at any rate, to whom rumour allotted him is not youthful, and is the mother of a son who has already achieved some distinction in public life. She is the widow of a peer, and has been a widow for a considerable time, but her at- tachment to Dr. Jameson is said to be as strong as his is to her. However, while his fate in connec- tion with the present trial remains uncertain, pru- dence probably dictates delay until the judicial pro- ceedings have run their course.

Lord Rosebery's urgent plea for a prompt inquiry in regard to the Chartered Company has helped to consolidate social opinion on the subject. It is now recognised that if the Government adhere to their original intention of not proceeding with the promised inquiry until next year, it will be of comparatively little use. There is reason to be- lieve that the leaders of the Opposition will take the earliest opportunity that presents itself to press the Government to order the inquiry forth- with. It is believed that the Colonial Secretary is Personally in favour of immediate action, but the Prime Minister is reported to be the leader of those Conservatives who prefer delay.

The particular service squadron which was called into existence just after the Kaiser's telegram to President Kruger is now about to be dispersed. It was on January Bth that the determination to com- mission the squadron was announced, so that it has been in existence about four months. It is inter- esting to know that at the Admiralty the way in which the squadron was mobilised is not regarded with the enthusiasm that it originally inspired among civilian observers. Although the announce- ment of the intention to commission was made on January Bth,it was not until the 14th that the admiral hoisted his flag, and not until the 20th that the squadron put to sea. It is the fashion to say that the squadron was fitted out in twenty-four hours and the Admiralty authorities regard this as much nearer the time which should have been occupied than the twelve days which actually were occupied.

'lhere is much interest, or, more strictls, curio- sity, felt at the action Mr. T. W. Russell is likely to takti if the Government surrender on the Irish Land Bill to the outcry of the Irish landlords. The belief is general that he will leave the Govern- ment, and seek a position of greater freedom on one of the benches below the Ministerial gangway. The Government stand pledged to Mr. Russell to pro- ceed with the Bill ; and Mr. Russell stands pledged to the Ulster tenant farmers that it will be pushed forward. Mr. Russell is a man neither of half- convictions nor of half-speech, and, as he is thoroughly sincere in his desire to act up to his pledges, he will not cling to office if the landlords are successful. The positicn of the Government is therefore a delicate one. They can carry the Bill through the Commons against the opposition of the landlords, but they run the risk of being beaten in the POllBB of Lords, where a Tory cave once before placed a Tory Government in an awkward plight.

The amendment paper relating to the Education Bill is becoming a volume of unwieldy proportions, and growing in size day by day. Every evening fresh showers of amendments are rained in upon tke clerks, and the calculations of one evening are left far behind by those of the next. At the be- ginning of the week no fewer than 1,300 or 1,400 amendments had been put down, and it is ex- pected that the number will be doubled before the committee stage is reached. It is manifestly im- possible to deal with such a list near the end of the session by the old ordinary process of patiently plodding through it, and between doing that and elosuring by compartments there is no alternative under the present rules of the house. By the latter process, some of the most important amendments will he completely blocked out without a single word of discussion, and as it will operate irrespec- tive of the claims of the one side or the other, the Ministerialists will suffer quite as much as the Opposition. Under the most favourable circum- stancet the Bill cannot be reached for some weeks, as the second reading of the Irish Land Bill is to be taken as soon as the Agricultural Ratingdlill passes through committee.

The Fadical Committee have now issued their manifesto. In the words of the Committee "it has been resolved to form a distinctive, advanced Radi- cal section in Parliament. and to appeal to the Radical elements in the Liberal party and in the constituencies to carry on an active and energetic campaign." In the opinion of the Committee it is the duty of the Radicals to unite their forces in the -.-flThrt to abolish the House of Lords and bring about a system of devolution,other wise Home Rule all round. It has been arranged, I believe, to hold a meeting of the new party every Tuesday after- noon, when Parliamentary action will be discussed and decided upon.

I hear that the Emperor William has recently developed a taste for the study of currency ques- tions, and that his Majesty has called for special reports, which are now being prepared, on the con- troversy in England. The latest works on bime- tallism and monometallism by English authors are to be sent to his Majesty.

The strike in the London building trade is break- ing down, bat almost the only fact that is known is that the collapse is arising from the retreat of the masters. Ninety mister joiners and carpenters have given way, and probably nearly the whole 10,000 of the workmen who struck work on Mon- day will be employed again this week. The masters have conceded the advance of wages, and are negotiating for a new code of work- ing rules. This leaves about 15,000 men still on strike but as negotiations have been begun with .ome Of' the other allied trades, it is believed that terms will be immediately arranged, and that the strike will be practically over before the week is out. But the deliberations are withheld from the Press with a scrupulousness that does not augur well for the spread of the principles of the new diplomacy.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY MAY 23, 1896 THE HIDDEN HAND;

" WOMEN'S CHAT."

At the Drawing Boom, held by the Princess of Wales last Monday, members of the Royal Family, and the wives of officials were still debarred from wearing colours,but the toilettes worn by the other women were, if possible, more brilliant than ever. The young Duchess of Marlborough was presented by her mother- in-law, the Marchioness of Blandford, and !oohed very lovely in her ivory satin gown trimmed with exquisite Brussels poiut. Her Grace carried a shower bouquet of lilies of the valley, and in addition to other handsome ornaments wore a very beautiful diamond girdle. The Duke and Duchess have now taken up their residence at 6, Audley-square for the season.

Passers-by would scarcely believe that such charming grounds as those attached to Buckingham Palace, lie hidden behind the ugly brick wall which stretches down Grosvenor-place. Yet,but for the rumbling of the street traffic,the picturesque spot might lie in the heart of the country. It is the one thing which reconciles her Majesty to an occasional brief sojourn at the palace. The shrubs, flowers and trees, are magnificent, and the swans,ducks and wild fowls on the lakes, and the pea- cocks on the lawns, make the scene a delightful one. The little son of Prince and Princess Adolphus of 'reek is just now on a visit to his grandparents,the Duke and Duchess of Teck at White Lodge, Richmond. It is satisfactory to know that there is every chance of the poor child's eight being restored to him, thanks to the excellent treatment which ho has received from his medical attendants. The two small sons of the Duke and Duchess of Ycrk are also staying with the Duke and Duchess of Teck,and there could not be a prettier spec- tacle than these three babies toddling about on the broad lawn which stretches behind the Lodge. Rumours are afloat concerning the approaching betrothal ef Queen Wilhelmina of Holland to Prince Bernhard Henry of Saxe Weimar, and although the young Queen will not be seventeen till August, there is every reason to believe that the report is trustworthy. The official announcement will, it is expected, be made on the 25th of next month, the 80th birthday of the Grand Duke of Saxe Weimar. Prince Bernhard Henry is only nineteen years of age. The little King of Spain, who was ten years old on the 17th inst., has now entered upon the dignity of one whole decade, by taking unto himself a separate house- hold from that of his mother, the Queen Regent. Con- gratulations were showered uppn the Queen Resent whose position has been a very responsible one during the past ten years. She has indeed cherished her fragile little boy's health with matronly solicitude, and so trained him morally and physically, that be will enter upon his public career,with every prospect of prosperity and renown.

It appears the Dowager Czarina of Russia intends to make Princess Maud a most magnificent pregent of jewels on her wedding. This the Dowager Empress is perfectly well able to do, as her collection is one ot the finest in the world. The late Czar was never tired of gratifying her taste for precious jewels, and he spent an enormous sum in this direction. Hardly second to the jewel casket of the Dowager Empress of Russia,how- ever, stands that of the Empress of Austria, whose black pearls are noted throughout Europe for their ex- treme beauty and rarity. But to return to my subject, viz., Princess Maud's wedding gifts, of the Princess of Wales's presents to her daughter will be an exquisitely worked copy of the Sacred Cross,which was found is the tomb of Queen Dagmar. When the Princess of Wales was married, her father the King of Denmark, gave her a copy of this cross,which is regarded with superstitious , veneration in Denmark.—Apropos of weddings, one may ' really begin to ask if a church is a suitable place in which ,to indulge in load laughter, plenty of talk, and not a I little Eirtation ; for it seems to be an established fact I that at weddings, churches are no longer either s-lemn or serious places. Several times lately I have wondered to find the exquisite singing and solemn service all un- heeded by the fluttering crowds of friends who have as- sembled to see two made into one.

Many of my readers who are not already aware of the fact may be interested to know that the practice of a woman changing her name on marriage originated from a Roman custom. The practice, however, is not universal. In many parts of the United States, a woman never relinquishes her maiden name, and is called by it as often as by that of her husband's. In many parts of France and Belgium the husband and wife's names are used together when either of them are mentioned, and in Spain and Portugal married women do not take the name of their husband, but continue to be known by their own. Even in the rural parts of England one often finds a married woman called by her maiden name, and in the country districts of Scotland it is sometimes found that both names are used.

There is no doubt that if the impecunious woman cannot ring the changes so necessary in evening gowns at the present mornent,when the London season is in full swing, she never will, for not only are fichus adorable, quaint and eminently useful, to be correct, but we are to be permitted the choice of either a contrasting bodice or contrasting sleeves. Speaking of the impecunious woman reminds me of an excellent recipe for cleaning black silk or satin, that, alas, has become shiny. Take some per- fectly clean potato parings, cover them with water.and allow them to soak twenty-four hours. Then strain, and well sponge the material with the water. Lay be- tween clean cloths, and iron, on the wrong side, until quite dry.

All the newest silk petticoats are daintily finished with an inner frill of pinked out shot silk,and an outer flounce of lace, either black or butter color. White petticoats are reserved for very light gowns, and frequently these are lined with a color. The safest shade in petticoats,to indulge in just now, is beige, as it goes with the fash- ionable grass lawn and with the endless shades of pale brown, tan, biscuit, deep cream, and buff tones, now so much en-evidence.

With regard to sunshades, the inside of this most use- ful complement of the toilette,is receiving quite as much attention this season as the outside. It is covered with gathered chiffon and lace frills and made beautiful in a variety of ways. This fashion we all cinnot but appre- ciate, for it is seldom a parasol is held upright, and the spectacle of unadorned ribs is not fascinating. I received a shock a few mornings back from the head- _ _ _

ini.Oi-iin--articlein one of our fiendon daily papers, "The Moon One Yard Off." Surely. I thought, this is too near! However on reading further I discovered that Mr. Deloncle, who is responsible for the telescope which is to achieve this wonder at the next Paris Exhibition, has now lengthened the distance to thirty-eight mi;es. In other words the moon will then be about the same distance from the earth as Hind Head is from London.

Philosophers have remarked that upon our best things we chance, and how true this ia ! For example,the other night a meteor appeared ,flashed a moment and was gone, but not however before several readers of a London evening newspaper had caught sight of it. Among these was a cyclist, who saw it while" keeping a sharp look out fvr stones." He observed that it was very beautiful that "its trail was marked by brownish smoke," and "just before it disappeared it divided into two." For- tunate cyclist, had he been seeking meteors, he would have found only stones. BOILED BATTER PUDDING i 5 delicious and very econom- ioal. Take two eggs, half a pint of milk, four ounces of flour, and some golden syrup. Beat the eggs and strain them inte the milk. Put the fiber in a basin and mix in the milk and eggs gradually, keeping the batter quite stiff at first. Pour into a greased pudding basin and boil for an hour. Heat two tablespoonfuls of golden syrup and pour it round the pudding as a sauce.

" MADGE."

CAPITOLA THE MADCAP. By Nils. E. D. E. N. SOUTHYTORT H.

CHAPTER XXXIX. CAP FREES THE CAPTIVE. •" Hold daughter ! I do spy a kind of hope, Which craveth as desperate an execution As that is desperate, which we would prevent And if thou darest, I'll give the remedy ! Hold, then ! go home, be merry, give consent To marry Paris ! Wednesday is to-morrow !" —SHAKESPEARE. As the autumn weather was now very pleasant, Capitola continued her rides, and without standing upon ceremony, repeated her visit to the Hidden House. She was as usual followed by Wool, who kept at a respectful distance, and who during hi mistress's visit remained outside ha attendame upon the horses.

Capitola luckily was in no danger of encounter- ing Colonel Le Noir, who since the night of the mysterious tragedy had not returned home; hut had gone to and settled in his winter quarters at Washington City. But she again met Craven Le Noir, who, con- trary to his usual ' custom of accompanying his father upon his annual migrations to the metropo- lis, had upon this occasion remained home in close attendance upon his cousin, the wealthy orphan. Capitola found Clara the same sweet, gentle and patient girl, with this difference only - that her youthful brow was now overshadowed by a heavy trouble which could not wholly be explained by her state of orphanage, or her sorrow for the dead --it was too full of anxiety, gloom and terror to have reference to the past alene. Capito'a saw all this, and trusting in her own powers, would have sought the confidence of the poor girl, with the view of soothing her sorrom and helping her out of her difficulties ; but Miss Day, candid upon all other topics was stranely reserved upon this subject, and Capitola, with all her eccentricity, was too delicate to seek to intrude upon the young mourner's sanctuary of grief. But a crisis was fast approaching which rendered further concealment difficult and dangerous, and which threw Clara for protection upon the courage, presence of mind and address of Capitola. Since Clara Day had parted with her betrothed and taken up her residence beneath her guardian s roof she had regularly written both to Traverse at St. Louis, and to his mother at Staunton. But she had received no reply from either mother or son. And months had passed filling the mind of Clara with anxiety upon their account. She did not for one moment doubt their con- stancy ; alas ! it required but littls perspicacity on her part to perceive that the letters on either side must have been intercepted by the Le Noire— father and son. Her great anxiety was lest Mrs. Rocke an.l Traverse, failing to hear from her, should imagin,, that she had forgotten them. She longed to asaare them that she had not! But how should she do this —lt was perfectly useless to write and send the letter to the post-office by any servant at the Hidden House, for such a letter so sent would be sure to find its way—not into the • mail-bags, but —into the pocket of Colonel Le Noir. Finally, Clara resolved to entrust honest Cap. with so much of her story as would engage her interest and co-operation, and then confide to her care the letter to be placed in the post-office. Clara had scarcely come to this resolution ere, as we said, an imminent crisis obliged her to seek the further aid of Capitola. Craven Le Noir had never abated his unaccep- table attentions to the orphan heiress. Day by day, on the contrary, to Clara s unspeakable dis- tress, these attentions grew more pointed and alarming. At first she had received them coldly and re- pulsed them gently ; but as they grew more ardent and devoted she became colder and more reserved, until at length by maintaining a freezing hauteur, at variance with her usually sweet temper, she sought to repel the declaration that was ever ready to fall from his lips. But notwithstanding her evident abhorrence of his suit, Craven Le Noir persisted in his purpose. And so, one morning, he entered the parlour and finding Clara alone, he closed the door, seated him- self beside her, took her hand and made a formal declaration of love and proposal of marriage, urging his suit with all the eloquence of which he was master.

Now Clara Day, a Christian maiden, a recently bereaved orphan and an affianced bride, bad too profound a regard for her duties towards Cod, her father's will, and her betrothed husband's rights, to treat this attempted invasion of her faith in any other than the most deliberate, serious and (iig- nitied manner.

"1 am very sorry, Mr. Le Noir, that it has at length come to this. I thought I had conducted myself in such a manner as totally to discourage any such purpose as this which you have just honoured me by disclosing. Now, however, that the subject may be set at rest for ever, I feel bound to announce to you that my hand is already pliuhted," said Clara, gravely. "But my fairest and dearest love, your little hand cannot be plighted without the consent of your guardian, who would never countenance the impudent pretensions which I understand to be made by the low born young man to whom, I pre- sume, you allude. That engagement was a very foolish affair, my dear girl, and only to be palliated upon the ground of your extreme childishness at the time of its being made. You must forget the whole matter, my sweetest love, and prepare your- selftp listen to a. suit more worthy of your social position," said Craven Le Noir, attempting to steal his arm around her waist. Clara coldly repelled him, saying: "I am at a loss to understand, Mr. Le Noir, what act of levity on my part has given you the assurance to offer me this affront ?" "Do you call it an affront, fair cousin, that I lay my hand and heart, and fortune, at your feet? "I have called your act, sir, by its gentlest name. Under the circumstances I might well have called it an outrage f' "And what may be those circumstances that eonvert an act of—adoration—into an outrage, my sweet Cousin?" Sir, you know them well! I have not concealed from yourself or. my guardian that I am the affianced bride of Doctor Roche, nor that our troth wtts plighted with the full consent of my dear fatherr, said Clara, gravely. "Tut, tut, tut, my charming cousin, that was mere child's play—a school girl's romanti: whim; do not dream that your guardian will ever permit you to throw yourself away upon that low-bred fellow !" "Mr. Le Noir, if you permit yourself to address me in this manner, I shall feel compelled to retire. I cannot remain here to have my honoured father's will and memory, and the rights of my betrothed, insulted in my person !" said Clara, rising to leave the room. " No—stay ! forgive me, Clara pardon me, Fentlest girl, if, in my great love for you. I grow impatient of any other claim upon your heart, especially from such an unworthy quarter ! Clara, you are a mere child, full of generous but romantic sentim-nts, and dangerous impulses ! You require extra vigilance and titan exercise of authority on the part of your guardian to save you fromeertain self-destruction! And some day, sweet girl, you will thank us for preserving you from the horrot s of such a mesalliance, said Craven Le Noir, gently detaining her. "I tell you, Mr. Le Noir, that your manner of speaking of my betrothal is equally insulting to myself, Doctor Roche, and my dear father, who never would have plightod our hands had he con- sidered our prospective marriage a mesalliance." 'Nor do I suppose he ever did plight your hands —while in his right senses !" " Oh sir ! this has been discussed be'ore ! I beg of you to let the subject drop for ever, remember- ing that I hold myself sacredly betrothed to Tra- verse Roche. and ready--when, at my legal majority, he shall claim me—to redeem my plighted faith by becoming his wife." " Clam-a! this is madness !it must not be en- dured, nor shall not! I have hitherto sought to win your heart by showing you the great extent of love ! but be careful how you scorn that love, or continue to taunt me with the mention of an un- worthy rival I for though I use gentle ?mom, should I find them fail of their purpose, I shall know how to avail myself of harsher ones." Clara disdained reply, except by permitting her clear eye to pass over him from head to foot, with an expression of consuming acorn that scathed him to the quick. "I tell you to be careful, Clara Day! I come to you armed with the authority of your legal guar- dian, my father, Colonel Le Noir, who will fore- stall your foolish purpose by throwing yourself and your fortune away upon a beggar, even though to do so, he strain his authority and coerce you into taking a more suitable companion,' said Craven Le Noir, rising impatiently, and pacing the floor. But no sooner had he spoken these words than he saw how greatly he had injured his cause, aud re.

THINGS A WOMAN WANTS TO KNOW.

uERTAINLY, with the advance of summer, capes are curtailed in length; some ladies seem to think the shorter they are the smarter, and so much reduce their length as to make them quite unbecoming. This is not the case, however, with the cape of which I am giving you a sketch. It was made of a dull shot red and green silk, trimmed with grass muslin, and edged with a deep frill of black chiffor.. It is cut full, as you will see, and falls in flutes, each flute being marked by a light passementerie of embroidered grass lawn. The high stand-up collar is of the same material, as are also the ends which reach to the lower edge of the cape. A wide bow of ribbon fastens it at the neck, and completes a very dainty garment, and one which, with a good pattern such as we supply, could be undertaken by any lady for herself. These little capes of silk, chiffon, and lace, are really such dainty making that they might be looked upon in the light of fancy work almost more than dressmaking, and a lady with good taste and nimble fingers will soon find that many of the trimmings she has by her can be utilised for their ornamentation.

The frill round this cape is put on quite at the edge, so that it is transparent. Many of the capes this year are, I consider, over- ornamented. Some of those which have bA.en

A Fashionable Cape. No. 3123. Paper Pattern, sp. To be had from PATTERN DEPARTMENT,IB, HENRIETTA. STREET LONDON, W.C.

seen in the Park lately have almost erred on the side of good taste, ribbons, laces, chiffon, and artificial flowers even, having been brought into requisition for their ornamentation, and this, too, with no light hand. The result is that in the struggle after what is considered fashion, many have forgotten that good taste must be the foundation of all really fashionable dressing. My second sketch shows a design which I trust will be very useful for an underskirt. This pattern is exceedingly well cut, and thoroughly suited for wearing under the full skirts of to-day. It is suitable for almost every material. First and foremost, of course, silk would be better than anything else, and in this case the frills—which, you will notice, are deeper at the back than in the front—should be edged with lace, and the top one gathered with a heading. For smart evening wear, white China silk, with frills edged with Valen- ciennes, leaves nothing to be desired. It is absolutely ideal wear, it washes perfectly, and will not easily wear out. To come to a more everyday petticoat, a fine white cambric trimmed with embroidered frills, or frills of cambric edged with fairly strong lace, or again, a zephyr of pale colouring with frills to match, would both look remarkably well? Some people I have seen lately have made their skirts of a pretty shot alpaca, and they had the frills only of silk. In this way the

A Pretty Underskirt. No. 3124. Parer Pattern, Bicl.

To be had from PATTERN DEPARTMENT, 18, HENRIETTA STREET, LONDON, W.C.

desired frou-frou is obtainable at considerably less cost. Moreover, of course, the alpaca skirt wears better than the ordinary glace silk. I notice at the present time that all the smartest people are wearing hats rather than toques; in fact, for the moment, toques are hardly as popular as formerly. Hats of medium size with a small brim are the most favoured this year, and these are trimmed with masses of tulle or gauze, and flowers of almost every kind. I notice the brims are still turned up considerably at the back to show bunches of flowers or rosettes of ribbon and lace nest- ling on the hair. Many of us now are enjoying to the full all kinds of outdoor summer games, tennis, boat- ing, golf, cycling, all come in for their share, and with what result? Of course our faces are considerably sunburnt, and our hands too. Many people do not mind the former, but the latter are not to be borne. I think, therefore, a simple lotion for whitming the hands may be appreciated by you. Place a pint of orange- flower-water, or rose-water, in a bottle. Add to it one ounce of glycerine, and a teaspoonful of powdered borax. Mix these well together, and use constantly after washing. It may also be used occasionally on the face, but it is not advisable to use it regularly, on account of the glycerine contained in its composition. Muslin curtains have a very brightening effect in a house during the summer, and care- ful housewives are glad to take down their handsome winter curtains, which readily spoil if exposed to the full glare of the hot summer sun, and replace them with muslin. Then again there are windows for which one cannot afford winter curtains, which yet may , be adorned by those of muslin. Let me ' recommend to you a very cheap kind of curtain pole. Secure an ordinary bro.-im handle and two brass knobs from your ironmonger. Paint the broom handle any colour to suit your woodwork, and then screw on the brass ends. With a dozen rings large enough to slip over the rod, and two long hooks to hold it, the curtain pole will be ready for its new and. dainty muslin curtains. Failing the broom- handle, bamboos, which can be bought quite inexpensively, will do as well.

Grass lawns grow more bewilderingly lovely every week, and made up over heliotrope satin or sateen, this particular fabric is delightful for blouses. Embroidery, too, made up over a color, is being used with much en- thusiasm to form blouses and in mcst of the West End shops at the present moment it is possible to buy pieces (specially cut, and traced to shape) which can easily be maniputatel by the home dressmaker.

And with these words, Clara bent her head and; passed from the room. Could she have seen the malignant scowl and gesture with which Craven Le Noir followed her departure, she would scarcely have tru.ted his expressions of penitence. Lifting his arm above his head, he fiercely shook his fist after her, and exclaimed : "Go on, insolent girl, and imagine- that you have humbled me! but the tune shall be changed by this day month ! for before that time, whatever power the law gives the husband over his wife and her property, shall be mine over you and your pos- sessions! He had an ugly way of scowling and biting his nails when deeply brood- ing over any subject, and now he walked slowly up and down the floor with his head. upon his breast, his brows drawn over his nose, and his four fingers between his teeth, gnawing away like &wild beast, while he muttered: "-She is not like the other one! she has more sense and strength! she will give us more trouble. LIFE IN THE FIREPLACE.

The object lecsons we have in life are so numerous and familiar that we naturally pass them by without notice. As we iaok at an open fire, for instance, few of us think of the principle of human life which is there revealed. Wilun the fire burns low, we throw on a litt#o kindling, and think no more about it. The fire soon burns brighty again, and the lesson is unheeded. When you notice that the fire of life burns feebly,why tdo you not remember the kindling ? Here is a lady— well known in the community in which she lives—who learned how to kindle this human flame. She writes :

"In glancing through the testimonials in a pamph- let, I read with interest cue which (=responded to the case of my little girl new fifteen tuonths old. At six months she was a really beautiful baby, but after she was weaned I was greatly disappofiated at the little pro- gress she made. She was not is any way ill, and was decidedly agood-tempered child., but made ibsolutely no progress, although I devoted •inv entire time to her. I cJuld not think it right that 41 child so well taken care of, and so much in the fresh air, should lose flesh in spite of apparent good health. When she was seven months old she had cut four , teeth, but in the next five months cut only one more. At this time when baby was a year old, I began giving her Scott's Emulsion. I have much pleasure in saying that she improved in a marvellous manner. Baby took the Emulsion with evident enjoyment, and I may say it was just what she needed to give her flesh, strength, and vitality. —Yours very truly, (signed), MRS. GEORGE HAN- DEL, Marsden, Whitstable, Kent, December, 19th, 1895."

The point to emphasise in Mrs. Handel's letter is that, although she devoted her entire time to her baby, it did not make any progress. There was no evidence of disease or any tangible ailment, but it was none the less evident that something was wanting. You have read her letter. You know what started the fire burning brightly again. It was kindling. There are probably millions of babies in the world who are not making any progress. They all need Scott's Emulsion to restore the blush of health and make dimples when they laugh. If you are a father or mother, you know what rosy cheeks mil dimples mean.

THE EXAMINER, SNTURDAY, MAY 23, 189 a

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

MONDAY.

in the House of Commons on Monday, Sir J. Gonsr,in answer to several questions, said it would not be prac- ticable to introduce clauses dealing with the superannua- tion of teachers into the Education Bill; and the Govern- ment did not see their way to introduce a Teachers' Pension Bill until after the provisions of the Education Bill had been settled by the House. In reply to Mr. P. A. M'Huou, Mr. BALFOUR said the question of an amnesty to the Irish treason-felony ,prisoners had already been discussed this session, and the decision of the Home Secretary given upon it. He did not, therefore, think it would be possible under pre- sent circumstances to renew a debate on the same sub- ject. Sir W. WEDDERBURN asked the Secretary for India if he would disallow the Cotton Duties Act. 1896, and the Act to amend the Indian Tariff Act .if 1894, on the ground that they increased the burden of taxation on the poorest class in India,while lightening the burden on those who were comparatively well-to-do. Lord G. fLunurox said the additional taxation upon a very small proportion of cotton cloth consumed in India repre- sented an infinitesimally small increase of cost to the in- dividual purchaser, while, on the other hand, the duty on yarns had been abolished. Under all the circum- stances he did not propose to advise the Government of , India to remit the duties recently imposed by the Legis- lature. Not regarding the answer as satisfactory, Sir W. Wedderburn moved the adjournment of the House. The whale of the Opposition, except the front beach, ' rose in support of the motion, and Sir W. WIDDERBURN proceeded to call attention to the effect of the Indian cotton duties as recently rearranged, which were said to be "causing grave dissatisfactiou in India by increasing the burden of taxation imposed on the poorest classes of consumers." Sir J. Leng seconded the motion which, after statements by Lord G.Hamilten and Sir H.Fowler, was withdrawn.

===== 0002601_18960523_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY.

In the Reuse of Commons on Tuesday, the London and North-Western Railway Bill was ordered for third reading, the Speaker having ruled out of order motions relating to third-class accommodation between Euston and Holyhead. Replying to Mr. MaIIGAN, Sir M. WatTs RIDLEY said he had no evidence that the shooting of homing pigeons was systematic, ner bad he reason to believe the police required to be reminded of their duty in the matter. Mr. CHAMBERLLIE informed Mr. Edmund Robertson that the form of the inquiry which the Government pro- posed to institute into the conduct of the British South Africa Company had onot yet been finally decided upon. In committee on the Agricultural Rating Bill, con- sideration of Mr. LuttrelPs amendment to tzeinpt from half the rates agricultural buildings as well as agricul- tural lands was resumed. Sir WILLIAI2. HABCOLIET ridi- culed the proposal, and said it was calculated to benefit the landlords. The .GSANCELLOft of the Exchmiultit declined to accept the amendment.which was negatived by a majority of 273. Mr. Ronsox proposed an amend- ment restricting the relief given by the bill to land let at an annual rent of less thanl an acre,but after some dis- cussion this was negatived by a majority of 63. Mr. Beraroug then moved that the words down to "pay one- half"stand out of the bill,and this was finally carried by a majority of 57. Mr.D.T.IIOMAS moved to leave out "one halt" and insert three-quarters, the object being to re- quire the occupiers of agricultural land to pay three quarters instead of one-half of the present rate. This was ,negatived by a majority of 76.

In the House of Lords, the Reserve Forces Bill was read a second time, and the Elouse went into committee on the Divorce Amendment Bill. An amendment to Clause 1 by Lord Gricathorpe to prohibit the remarriage of persons found gnilty of adultery by a superior court was negatived without a division, and the bill after- wards passed through committee. The Locomotives on Highways Bill and the Derelict 'Vessels Report Bill also passed through committee.

WEDNESDAY.

The Agricultural Land Rating Bill was again ccnlsi" dered in Committee in the House of COMMOIRS on Wed- nesday. On clause 1 Mr. Lloyd-George ved a n amendment to provide that relief should be given only mo in the case of land out of which, owing to depression in agriculture, no rent had been derived for the three years next before the passing of the Act. The amend- ment was rejected by 190 votes to 82. The Chairman (Mr. J. W. Lwther) then ruled a page and a half of amendments out of order on the ground that they pro- posed alterations in the general law of assessment and the law of landlord and tenant, and also that the Com- mittee had already decided in the bill that the occupier should only be Habil to half the rate. Mr. Lloyd- George moved an amendment to except the poor rate from the operation of the bill. The amendment was supported by Sir H. Fowler and Sir W. Har- court, both of whom described the proposal in the bill as a first step towards a national poor rate. After further debate Mr. BALFOUR moved the closure. which was carried by 238 votes to 119, and the amend- ment was rejected by 246 votes to 112. Two minor amendnaents having been withdrawn, the question was put that clause 1 stand part of the bill. Mr. J. A. PEASE moved its rejection. Mr. BALFOUR again moved the closure, which was carried by 249 votes to 117, and the motion that the clause should stand part of the Bill was carried by 248 votes to 111. The clause was then agreed to, progress was reported, and the House resumed.

PADGATE.

NOT RAVING CONTROL.-At the Warrington County Petty Sessions, on Wednesday, William Owen, Warring- ton, was fined is. and costs for not having proper con- trol of his horse at Poulton on the 14th May by being 150 yards from it.—A similar charge was brought Against Thomas Tomlinsan,who said that he was deliver- ing bread at a house 150 yards away and the wheel of his cart was locked for safety.—Mr. John Eck.rsley, Warrington, defendant's employer, said there was no nearer way ot getting to the house in question without leaving the cart. He did not consider there was any danger as the wheel of the vehicle was locked on each occasion.—The magistrates ordered defendant to pay the costs.--James Rafter was charged with committing a similar offence at Poulton by being 30 yards from his horse on the 30th April.—He pleaded guilty and was fined is. and costs.

An inquest was held on Wednosday on the body of Alice Allison,the young woman who was shot by her husband,the manager of the Rising Sun public-house in the Strand,London. It was stated that two brothers of Allison had committed suicide,and that he said on Sun- day night he had gone out one day to do as his brother,, had done. Miss Archer,barmaid,said Mr. and Mrs. Alli- son did not appear to agree very well. The cook at the Rising Sun spoke to a complaint made by the deceased, and that her husband took more notice of Miss Archer than of her. A police constable dr posed that Allison told him he shot his wife through jealousy. The inquiry was adjourned. Allison is still in the hospital suffering from a gunshot wound self-inflicted after the murder of his wife.

NOTES AND NEWS.

Flower farming undertaken by women ir by no means unlucrative. One lady is doiwg well with a violet farm and apple orchard. Another lady, no longer young, but with an indomitable pluck, compelled by circumstances to make her own living, started a lily farm in a remote Fu sex village. She is succeeding wonderfully, and ebides her lilies, disposes of eggs, cream, mushrooms, and other garden produce without difficulty. She was, too, a London woman, who had had no previous experi- ence in country ways and doings.

Mark Twain's home in Hartford is a sort of pilgrim's shrine. Not long since a caller, a big, good-natured countryman—a butcher, as he introduced himself—after a few minutes' chat, asked Mark, 6' Now tell me for a fact, are you the one that wrote all them books ?" "Truly I am," said Mark. "Of course you are ! Of course you are !" cried the honest fellow ; "but, by George, I shouldn't think it from your looks !" Whereat Mark was hugely tickled, says the author of a " Cen- tury " character-sketch.

A Mormon choir is travelling on the Pacific Coast. Two daughters, one son-in-law, and several grandchil- dren of Brigham Young are members of the choir. They have one hundred and seventy•five singers.

Paderewski never signs an agreement, and he has never failed to keep an engagement except through serious illness. It is a peculiarity of his that he cannot pass a blind man without giving him alms.

A brakesman on the Erie Railway, a few days since, caught a spark on the back of his celluloid collar as his train entered a station : the collar took fire and exploded with a loud report. He seized the collar with both hands and tore it from his neck,and thereby was burned severely on the face, neck, and hands. Be will be dis- abled for some time.

A correspondent of the " Daily Chronicle "writes : " I have good reason to believe that the key of famous cipher despatches fell into the hands of the Boer Govern- mens as the result of a curious accident. Among the Johannesburg prisoners in Pretoria Gaol is a Mr. Wet,or e,e %Vet (White ?). Not long ago a criminal confined in the gaol having served his time was discharged, and on departing took with him Mr. de Wet's overcoat instead of his own. He soon found cut his mistake, but his at- tention was attracted to what appeared to be a quantity of papers sewed in the lining. In the hope, perhaps, that it was money in the form of banknotes, he ripped open the lining, and discovered, not banknotes, but the long-lost key of the cipher despatches which the Trans- vaal officials had so long been searching for."

In a certain fashionable and well-known West-end thoroughfare an elderly but energetic individual may be seen sweeping a crossing from half-past ten in the morning until noon, and again from seven to eight p.m. At first sight there would seem to be nothing startling in this, but a certain romantic and pathetic interest at- taches to it from the fact that the crosing-sweeper is related to two well-known public men, both in Parlia- ment, one of them a Junior Lord of the Treasury, and that having no other means of subsistence he has chosen the honourable, if humble, method of livelihood des- cribed above, and has selected for his" pitch" that por- tion of the thoroughfare which leads from the front door of one of these more happily situated relatives.

Lord Wolseley has jest issued a series of amen.iments of the Volunteer Regulations,amongst which there is an addition to the enrolment procedure as follows : " Every person offering himself for enrolment will be re- quired to pass a physical examination, which will be carried out by a medical officer of the corps. The chief points to be attended to in the examination are—(a) That his vision is sufficiently good to enable him to see clearly with his naked eye at the required distance, as laid down on Army Form I. 1,220. (b) That his hearing is good. (c) That his chest is capacious and well formed, and that his heart and lungs are sound." The regulations also state that the £2O outfit allowance for subalterns will be issued half on appointment and half after a month's successful instruction at a school or with the regular forces; that officers attending schools of in- struction,or with regular troops,will be allowed 8;. a day (10s. for submarine miners) ; that officers' allowance with marching columns,or in Cann), will be 4s. a day,in- stead of 2g.; and that in exceptional cases of corps or detachments desiring to encamp with regular forces at a distant military station,the Secretary for War will con- sider applications for the actual cost of travelling, if the corps make specially economical arrangements for their conveyance.

A Belgian syndicate has purchased a vast tract of territory in the south of Argentine for the purpose of colonisation.

The" Revue Anglo-Romaine " states that the Golden Rose annually blessed at Easter by the Pope and sent to that one of the Catholic princesses who has particularly distinguished herself during the year for her devotion to the Chutch is to be sent to the Princess of Bulgaria," to recompense her for the noble firmness with which she endeavoured to prevent the apostasy of her husband."

Among his many other attributes, Shakespeare seems to have been very strongly prophetic. At the New York Electrical Exhibition on Saturday a telegraphic message was sent round the world in fifty minutes! Mr. Chau- ncey Depew wrote a despatch as follows :—" God created Nature's treasures and science utilizes electric power for the grandeur of the nations and the peace of the world." This was sent by President Chandler,of the Postal Tele- graphs Department, who was sitting at one side of a table in the Exhibition, and opposite him, at the other side of the table was Mr. Edison waiting t ) receive it after its long journey. Mr. Chandler transmitted it at 8.34 over the wires of the Western Union, through Chicago, Los Angeles, San Francisco, and thence to Van- couver,Winnineg,Montreal, Canso, and London,which it reached at 8.38. Thence it was sent through Lisbon, Gibraltar, Malta, Alexandria, Suez, Bombay, Madras, Singapore, Shanghai, Nagasaki,and Tokio. The message was returned by the same route,and was received by Mr. Edison in fifty minutes after Mr. Chandler had started it. The message was reported from each of the places mentioned as it reached it. This is the fastest piece of international telegraphinglever accomplished. The mes- sage cost at tariff rates 152 dots. 52 cents.

William Liebknecht, who has just come over to Eng- land on a lecturing tour' is, and always has been, one of the leading Socialists in Germany. Unlike Babel, his re- doubtable companion in arms—who only enjoyed a vil- lage school education, and is a turner by trade—Lieb- knecht studied at the university of Giessen,where he was born in 1826, and afterwards t Berlin and Marburg. Moreover, whereas Bebel only became converted to Socialism late in life, Leiblmecht may be said to have been a man of war in the cause from his youth upwards —either with pike or pen. In the revolutionary year 1848 he joined the Baden iasurgents,for which he subse- quently served a few months' imprisonment. After that, until 1862,he led a fugitive life in Switzerland and Eng- land, where,llke Kossuth, he acquired the mastery over our tongue which enables him to address British audi- ences as fluently almost as if they were German. His in- tercourse with Karl Marx in England intensified his Socialist convictions, and he hastened to join the move- ment organised by Lassalle.

A Russian paper states that no fewer than forty-nine lady singers, each of prima-donna rank, were engaged for the Coronation festivities. The fees paid to Mme. Sigrid Arnoldsou alone are said to be more than three thousand guineas for a short series. Saving Mme. Adelina Patti, no singer has commanded such terms in Russia.

ATTEMPTED SUICIDE BY A GIRL.

On Wednesday morning at the Warrington County l'etty Sessions, before Captain Withington, Mr. S. It. Armitage, and Mr. W. Houghton, Mary Harrison, aged 15, a pit-brow girl residing with a Mrs. Barrett, at Collins Green, was charged with attempting to commit suicide by drowning herself in a pond at Burtonwood on the 17th May. Inspector Morgan said that the girl was 15 years of age and was living with her grandmother. Lately she had been courting a young man who had left her. The defendant had been teased about it, and she went and threw herself in a pit. He added that the girl had been in custody at his house since the 17th May and had seemed very penitent. A woman who lived with the girl's grandmother had promised to look after her. Thomas Makin, a fireman, living in Fa.lrclough-street, Burtonwoad, said that at 8 o'clock on Sunday night he was coming down Chapel-lane and saw three girls going over a style. Directly after he passed he heard a scream that someone was drowning. He immediately went to the spot and found the girl Harrison in a pond of water up to her neck. Witness got hold of her arms and pulled her towards the side. Defendant said " Don' pull me out. Let me drown. It's through Jack .71 He persuaded the other girls to take her home. Defendant promised the bench not to act so foolishly again and was discharged.

WARRINGTON PA RK.

This park is just now looking at its best, and is well worth a visit. When it is considered that the park is situated in the centre of a smoky town, the wonder is that the trees, shrubs, and flowers flourish so well. Great credit is due to Mr. Parker, the head gardener,for his judicious choice of plants suitable to the situation, and for the excellent manner in which the park is kept. Several improvements have been made, and a few par- ticulars respecting them may prove interesting. Entering by the new gate opposite Parker-st., it will be noticed that the plot of ground near the palisadings bordering Sankey-st., has been made quite trim and neat. It has been planted with laburnum, privet, elder and lime trees, and also a number of plants in- cluding the iris. It is bordered by a newly- laid verge about three feet wide. Right opposite this entrance is a mound of Foil which was tipped here at the time the wall was taken down. This has now been laid out and a number of privet trees planted thereon. The opening of this additional entrance has created the need for asphaltic walks at this end of the park,the present scattered material on the walks present- ing a ragged appearance in contrast to the other walks in the vicinity. On the outer circle round the band- stand,a row of limes, about ninety in number,have been placed at a distance of about six yards apart, and look in a very healthy condition. Proceeding up the walk running parellel with Crosfield-street, on the left hand side will be seen a newly-laid verge running from one end to the e,ther,nearly a quarter of a mile in length. The privets which were planted alongside the palisadings have grown to such a height as to screen the walk from the public when travelling along Crosfield-street, and the walk is now one of the finest and most select in the park. It may be stated that this and other walks are now placed at the disposal of lady-cyclists, and each morn- ing from 6 a.m. to 8.30 a.m. a number of ladies repair there with their " bikes." The recreation ground has beeu banked round so that it may be flooded in winter time. This should make an excellent skating place. Entering the park itself by the entrance nearest Ken- drick-street, an improvement will be noticed near what has been known as the vinery. The ground bas been nicely laid out, and tke raw tile which surrounded it has given place to a fine verge. Numerous beds of blood-red and yellow wall-flowers, look exceed- ingly pretty, and the air is scented with sweet smelling azalea, lilac, and laburnum trees. The rustic arches near to the aviary are now becoming covered with clinging plants, and are making quite a sheltered passage. Upwards of 3,000 plants, chiefly geraniums, will shortly be taken from the greenhouses and placed in beds. The aviary still continues to be a centre of at- traction, and this is well-looked after by Mr. Ashton. It is stocked with numerous birds, and additional speci- mens are always being added. Recently,there has been a marked increase in the number of paraquets, there being now about a score in the aviary.

PENKETH.

ST. MARS'S C.E.T.S.—On Friday evening last week the annual tea party to the members of the St. Mary's Church of England Temperance Society, was held in the Infants schoolroom. A very good tea was given and the meeting was much enjoyed by those present. _ _

S. MARY'S miNsTaiis.—On Saturday afternoon the members and friends of the St. Mary's amateur minstrel troupe held their first annual trip, their destination being Frodsham Hills. The minstrels assembled in full force under the able leadership of their popular interlocutor Mr. J. Thompson. The party numbered 40 in all and proceeded to their destination in one of Mr. Conn n's four-in-hand 'busses. The niggers were of course en eostumt, and were seated in a prominent position on the 'bus, and created no small amusement in the places they passed through. Songs and choruses enlivened the journey, and on arrival at the Bear's Paw the horses were stabled, and the troupe assembled in front and gave a slight performance prior to ascending the hill to Mersey View where they were expected. Arrived there they amused themselves in various ways until the tea was pronounced ready. A substantial tea was disposed of, and then the troupe took possession of the platform, and the services of a generous lady pianist having been secured, an entertainment was given. The members were in great form and the visitors present were greatly pleased .with their performance, which was a decided success. Prior to the home journey another perform- ance was given before the Bear's Paw, and was much enjoyed by the inhabitants. The home journey was accomplished without any untoward incident, Penketh being reached at 10.45. The trip was a signal success, and the thanks of all are due to Mr. P. Pownall for his efficient arrangement of the travelling department, and to Mr. E.Sutton andMr.J. Thompson,the energetic secre- tary and interlocutor, for the way they looked after the comfort of the party.

SERIOUS STREET ACCIDENT AT WA TIRINGTiiN

On Monday afternoon, about tour o'ciocKoi iictle girl, about two years old, who resides with her parents at Napier-at., was admitted into the In- firmary. She had been knocked down by a run- away horse near her home and seriously injured. The horse was stopped after colliding with a cart. DEATH OF MRS. R. GREENALL.

In the parish of Grappenhall and the surrounding country regret is keenly felt at the death of Mrs. Greenall, wife of the Rev. Richard Greenall, rector of Grappenhall, which took place on Tuesday, at the Rectory, Grappenhall. The decaased lady was 63 years of age and her loss will be felt by both the Rector and his people. PROPERTY SALE AT WARRINGTON.

On Tuesday evening, at the Corn Exchange, Blue Bell Inn, Horsemarket-st., Mr. Thomas Sutton offered for sale by auction, subject to the public sale conditicns of the Liverpool Law Society, 14 dwelling-houses, Nos. 1 to 27, Erwood-st., off Buckley-st., Winwick-road. The property is free- hold, contains 1,282 superficial square yards of land produces a gross anual rental of £146 18s., and is subject to a yearly chief rent of £25. The solicitors to the venders were Messrs. Edward Heath and Sons, Swan-st., Manchester. There was a fairly good attendance. The bidding s ;arted at £5OO and rose to £925, when the sale was declared an open one. Bids of £5 followed, and ultimately, the lot was knocked down to Mr. Fred. J. Carlisle,butcher, Market-st., for £960. NEWSOME'S CIRCUS, WARRINGTON.

A well-varied and attractive programme has been submitted at the circus in Queen-st. this week. The bears have again proved a great attrac- tion, and their performance has been one of the prime favourites of the whole programme. Mule- riding and the comic antics of the clowns have been the most amusing section of the entertain- ment, while the Yeldings have again been loudly applauded for their graceful and daring equestrian- ism. Acrobatic displays have been given by the Lizzetta troupe, and Miss Rubina has well pleased the audience by her agility OR the tight wire. A local-attraction was the display of boxing given by the Corbet Migets, boy and girl, and children of Mr. James Hunt, the famoua Warrington jumper. A three-round glove fight is engaged in by them, and in which both give evidence of a knowlege of the "noble art." The little girl is quite a match for her brother,and deals him some hard knocks. There are numerous other items of a pleasing character, and under Mr. Cook's directorehip Mr. James New- some's Circus provides good fun for Warrington pleasure-seekers. LOCAL MINI)7G CASE.—BANKS AND OTHERS V. AcKtEts, WHITLEY AND CO., LTD.

This case was heard in the Queen's Bench Division before Yr. Baron Pollock and Mr. Justice Bruce, on Monday and Tuesday, the 4th and sth May, when owing to the importance of the questions involved judgment was reserved. Mr. Lawson Walton, Q.C., and Mr. M'Swinney, instructed by Messrs. Jeans and Son, solicitors, Warrington and Manchester, represented the plaintiffs, and Mr. Robson, Q.C., and Mr. Darlington instructed by Messrs. Darling- ton and Son. solicitors, Wigan, represented the defendants, The plaintiffs are Mr. Thomas Banks, mining engineer, of Manchester, Mr. John Leyland Martlew, estate agent, of The Grange, Hindley, and Mr. Louis Adrian Voisey, chartered accountant, Warrington, as trustees of the will of the late Mr. Abraham Ackers, of Bickershaw Ball, near Wigan; and the defendants are Messrs. Ackers, Whitley,and Co., Ltd., of the Plank-lane Collieries, near Wigan. The main question to be decided was whether the defendants are or are not liable to pay the fixed minimum rent of £250 a year during the residue of a term of 21 years from the Ist May, 1875, they having shortly after that date closed the shafts Ithrough which the two seams of coal,the subject of the lease, were intended to be worked, or whether after having continued to pay the minimum rent Idown to Ist May, 1894, the defendants were justi- fied in declining to pay any further rent on the ground that they had, as they alleged, paid the full value of the coal, and in order to prove that they I had done so they claimed to be entitled to adduce in evidence the thickness of the same seams of coal, as they had been found to exist, in other or adjoining lands. On both these questions the I Court on Saturday last gave judgment in favour ot the plaintiff, with costs as against the defendants

SHOP FIRE IN G OLBORNE-EITRBET.

A fire broke out at the shop of Mr. David Owen, carver and gilder, and picture frame manufacturer, Golborne-street,on Saturday evening. About seven o'clock Mr. Frank Turner, the deputy-superintend- ent of the Warrington Fire Brigade, was passing down Golborne-street when be perceived smoke issuing from underneath the front door of Mr. Owen's shop. Being apprehensive, he immediately made inquiries as to the cause of the smoke, and found that the inside premises were in flames. With all possible speed he returned to the Fire Engine Station, and finding several of the brigade men on the spot, the alarm bell was not rung, it being feared that too large a crowd would conse- quently collect, and in such a narrow street would seriously hinder the operations of the firemen. The reel cart was promptly got out, and in a few minutes two connections were made, and volumes of water were poured on the burning building. It was discovered that the fire had attained a firm hold, but after working hard for about fifteen minutes, the firemen—who had in the meantime been joined by more of their comrades—were ob- taining the upper hand, although little hope was held out of saving much of the stock in the shop. The flames were soon effectually subdued, and had been successfully confined to the shop and rooms above and beyond, which were used for working and storing purposes. It is regarded as very for- tunate that the fire had not happened earlier in the week. Had it. occurred some four or five days before, the loss would have been much greater, for then several important and valuable orders were being executed. Most ot these, however had been finished and sent home by Saturday. The origin of the fire is unknown, but it is supposed to have commenced in the wmkshop at the back of the shop. About six o'clock in the evening Mr. Owen left everything apparently safe when te went to transact some business. He was returning home about two hours afterwards when the startling news of the occurrence was communicatecl to him. There was no one on the premises at the time, but Mr. Owen's family live next door to the shop, where the business will be carried on pending the work of reconstruction. The property is partially insured in the Union Fire Assurance Company. WARRINGTON VOLUNTEERS' ANNUAL CHURCH PARADE.

On Sunday morning the Warrington Volunteers attended at the Parish Church for Divine service a week prior to proceeding to camp. Assembling at the Drill Hall at 9.15 to the strength of about 300 they fell in and marched along Bath-st., Legh-at., Sankey-st., Buttermarket-at., Irlam-st., and Church- st, to the Parish Church, the band preceding them and playing appropriate selections of music. Colonel Ridgway was in command, and among the officers present were Captain and Adjutant Silver- thorne, Captains J. D. Buckton, Baynes' Appleton, Chorley, and Fairclough, Lieutenant Fairclough, Lieutenant Fletcher, Captain and Quartermaster Hutchinson, Sergeant-Major Sullivan,and Sergeant- Instructor Barker. The troop presented a smart and soldierly appearance which was much ad- mired. Having entered the sacred edifice the band played the National Anthem, following upon which the hymn "Fight the good fight" was sung, the band accompanying. The Rev. Canon Willis, rector, and acting-chaplain of the battalion, preached a brief and appropriate sermon. He took for his text 2 Timothy ii. 3-7 " Thou therefore endure hardness,as a good soldier of Jesus Christ. No man that warreth entangleth himself with the affairs of this life; that he may please Him who bath chosen him to be a soldier. And if a man also strive for masteries, yet is he not crowned, except be strive lawfully. The husband- man that laboured] must he first partaker of the fruits. Consider what I say; and the Lord give thee understanding in all things" The hymns "Onward, Christian Soldiers," and "Hail the day that sees Him rise" were subsequently sung, the band playing the accompaniments. At the conclu- sion of the service the men left the church, and , after being formed into" four deep "marched back to the Drill Hall. Prior to being disbanded, the Commanding Officer highly complimented them I upon their efficient appearance, and expressed his gratification that they had assembled in such large numbers. A small fatigue party, under the com- mand of Captain Baynes, ieft Warrington on Tues- day for the camp ground at Conway Marsh. Their duties will be to take over the ground and mark it out. Another fatigue party under the command of Captain and Quartermaster Hutchinson, consisting of about 30 men, left, on Wednesday morning re assist in pitching the tents in readiness for the arrival of the main body of the battalion this even- ing.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIETY.

The quarterly open meeting of this society was held on Sunday, and as on previous occasions it was largely attended. Mr. Zeba Armitage, J.P., presided, and la the course of a short address said that if they could see churches full to that extent every Sunday the reproach about their churche4 being half full would fall to the gtound. That large congregation appealed to him in rather a different manner. A few weeks ago when he was in Egypt such a sight as he saw that af,ernoon would have bee?: an impossibility. In E4ypt, except with those Englishmen who resided there for the purpose of business and the Wark done by the American Mission, Christianity was almost unknown. The people pro- fessed hardly any religion ; those who die were followers of Mello:inlet. But there were only a few who took an interest in religious work,and these were not ashamed to show it. It was quite com- mon to see the followers of Mahomet go through their devotions in the middle of the street or in public places. f hey were not hindered by tbe fear of being seen in rendering their worship. It was a. part of their ceremonial religion and the benefit they de- rived from their religion was connected with their -obser- vance of ceremonies. We had a different belief and did not believe in public and ostentatious ceremonies. But we might learn a lesson from these Mohammedans and that was that we should not be ashamed of the religion we professed. There were many times when they would be thrown into the company of people who themselves were not religious people and before whom it seemed difficult to maintain their religious character. - But those were times when they could show their courage pretty much in the same way as the Mohammedans. He thought that services of that description were a very great privilege to all of them. Public services, conducted in that way for the good of those who attended them, were quite different from some Mohammedan services which were for the bcnefit of the priests, the people tak- ing a small part in them. Their s-Tvices were of the heart in wliich they all rendered a real and hearty service. Miss Dutton, of Moore, delivered the address for the afternoon, and at the outset exrressed regret at the absence of the president of the society—lhe Rev. John Yonge—accl hoped he might be EOM with them again. She proceeded to speak of the parable of the "Prodigal Son," and drew many useful lessons from it. The ser- vice was also taken part in by the Rev. J. S. Hughes, of Hill Cliffe, Mr. B. Farrar, and Mr. T. Wallington. Mr. W. H.Walling,ton was tle solopt and he very effectively sang " The Cleansing Fires:' A 'cello solo was contri- buted by Mr. Edgar Balinford in a musicianly manner, and Mr. W. H. i'ayton presided at the organ with his. usual ability. A collection ,was taken on behalf of the P.S.A. funds.

STOCKTON HEATH.

THE REV W. S. HCILME'S DEPARTURE.

On Wednesday evening a social gathering was held ire the Chtgeli-room, Stockton Heath. Songs were given by several of the members and various games indulged in to the enjoyment of all. Opportunity was taken of the gathering to present to the Rev. W. S. Huline, who is leaving the parish, with " Sadler's Commentary on the New Testament" (12 vols.), as a souvenir from the members of the Church Institute and friends. Mr. H. N. Houghton made the presentation,and said they gave Mr. Hulme their best wishes in his new sphere of labour. —The Rev. W. S. Hulme, who was received with ap- plause,in expressing his gratitude for the gift, said he could hardly believe he had such kind frienas. He had been a very shert time stationed amongst them and he felt that he had done extremely little to deserve such a kind recognition on their part. It was a great pleasure to him to be connected with the Men's Club or in any way with his fellow men. In deciding upon those la volumes he could assure them that they had made a very wise selection and could not have chosen a better work or a more useful commentary. Alluding to his own work Mr. Hulme said they knew that a clergy- man's work was by no means an easy one. A clergy- man's lot was not a "bed of roses." It had some thorns as well. (Rear, hear.) They, the clergymen had great things expected of them, and it was not an easy thing to please everybody. Tlu y were criticised and taken to task, but, generally speaking, they tried their level best to please. They were not ministers of the people but ministers of Christ, and as such bad to minister to man, woman; and child. (Applause.) The speaker then ad- vised his hearers as to work they could do, and said that as members of the bible class or church room they could influence others, and had done, and it was to the credit of Mr. H. J. Westbrook and Mr. Wine that the class had risen to its present strength. (Ap- plause.) Mr. Hulme added that it was the greatest grief to him to leave, and be hoped he might come again at some future time. In his visits, of which he had made 730, he had always been received very kindly and although people might say that they had not seen him, that was bezause it. had not been in his power. These constant changes were bad for the parish—(hear, hear)—but they knew how they were brought about.—Mr. J. L iv/ton also spoke in terms of praise of Mr. Hulme, and hoped he would get a better situation elsewhere.—Mr. H, J. Westbrook dise!aimed any prominent participation with regard to the success of the Bible Class, taken by either himself or Mr.Daine. He believed in the difficult work of parsons, and said it was the duty of the parishioners to give all the sym- pathy possible to the clergymen, and not to criticise. He believed he was right in saying that the present repre- , seated their feelings towards Mr. Hulme. (Applause.) —Mr. Daine also spc.ke, and referred to changes being disadvantageous. They had no sootier got used to a Dian than they had to accustom themselves to someone else. —On the first page of Vol. 1 of the Commentary were the words :—" To the Rev. Walter S. Hulme. A souvenir, with complement of other volumes, from the members and friends of the Churhroom on his leaving St. Thomas', Stockten Heath. Whitsuntide, 1896." This page had been art stically illumined by Mr. S. F. Welsby.
