THE DUKE of DEVONSHIRE has The Duke of not been much to the front lately Devonshire. as a political speechmaker, but he again broke the ice, so to speak, at a meeting of Conservative delegates at Swansea on Wednesday. Perhaps his Grace's temporary retirement from the political platform was partly due to the fact that the speeches he made a few months ago have, in the light of subsequent events, turned out to bo rather unfortunate utterances. People do not for- get, for instance, that he promised the country a period of repose as a result of the return of a Unionist Government. As it happens, repose is the very thing which the country has not ob- tained. The speech of Wednesday was framed in a cautious key. There was no attempt made on this occasion to forecast the future and there were even one or two notable omissions from the defence of the Govornment's policy. No allusion V'as, for example, made to that extraordinary production of reactionary Toryism, the Agricultural Rating Bill, which the Government is forcing through the House of Commons by a constant use of the closure. On this subject the Dulut of DEVONSHIRE had nothing to say, and it is not at all improbable that he con- siders the less said the better it will be for his own party. But if Tory and Unionist speakers care- fully avoid this question there are, fortunately, plenty of other people who do not hesitate to make themselves heard in opposition. The Government should begin to realise the reprehensible character of their proposals when Town Councils are protest- ing against the Bill. This is what has been done by the Crewe Town Council, which is able to give very convincing reasons for the action it has taken. It is proposed by this Bill to hand over 2,000,000 a year to the owners of agricultural land, for there can be little doubt that the money will, soon er or later and directly or indirectly, find its way into the pockets of that small and privi- leged class. Roughly speaking, therefore, this means that the people of the country will be taxed at the rate of a shilling per head per annum for the benefit of rural landlords. Reckoned upon this basis the contribution of the borough of Crewe will amount to £2,000 a year. The Cor- poration of that town rightly protest against the exaction. It is pointed out that if the £2,000 were placed at the disposal of the Corporation "it would suffice to pay the annual instalment of principal and interest on the loan of £39,100 at three per cent. for thirty years," by means of which loan various useful and much needed improve-

ments might be carried out without any additional cost to the ratepayers. Then, again, it is pointed out that the agriculturists surrounding Crewe are really less in need of assistance than the towns- people themselves. The workmen are nearly all employed in the works of the London and North- Western Railway Company and these works have been on short time during the greater part of the last three years. The result is that even the agricultural labourers of the district have been in a better position than the workingmen in the borough. Yet the latter are to be called upon to contribute through the medium of the taxed articles they consume, and in other ways, for the benefit of the landlords and the farmers. The Rating Bill when viewed in the light thrown upon it by this petition is clearly seen to be a mon- strous piece of class legislation, and it is, perhaps, surprising that more Town Councils and other representative bodies in the urban districts have not protested against it. In a town like War- rington, for instance,with an estimated population of nearly 60,000,the contribution, on the basis of a shilling per head,amounts to nearly £3,000 a year. The Corporation of this town could certainly join with that of Crewe in saying that such a sum as that would enable them to carry out several very necessary improvements. The Rating Bill being what it is, there is nothing surprising in the fact that the DuKE of DEVONSHIRE did not deal with it, especially when addressing a town audience. The Duke is in some respects a plain blunt man, and it is not at all unlikely that, if he had referred to the Bill, he would have candidly admitted its true character—that it will be and was intended to be in the interests of the landlords. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S own colleagues have been singu- larly chary in awarding him praise for his methods of dealing with the South African difficulty. The Duke of DEVONSHIRE made some amends for this on Wednesday, though a few remarks on the latest developments would have been listened to with interest. It is evident that the whitewashing of the Chartered Compaq and its managing directors is not helping the prisoners in Pretoria. The four leading Johanesburcrers who were con- demned to death have had their sentences re- duced to fifteen years' imprisonment. This is still a more severe punishment than was expected, and it is believed that it will be further reduced.linfor- tunately, the English Government has not placed itself in a position to interfere with good effect. It has practically declined to even prosecute the

===== 0002601_18960523_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
TFIE EXAMINER SATURI )AY, MAY 23, 1896.

ringleaders in the plot to upset the Boer Govern- ment, and the latter will, therefore, almost feel bound to make its :punishments more severe than it would otherwise have done.

THE coronation ceremony which , The Czar's Coionation is being conducted on a very magnificent scale at Moscow, is naturally leading many people to ask what sort of man the new CZAR is. That is, however, a question which it is easier to ask than to answer. There is either not a great deal known about the Russian Autocrat or else what is known is rjot made public. One of the fullest biographies of the young ruler is that given by Mr. STEAD in this month's " Review of Reviews," but even this account falls far short of satisfying public curio- sity. In one respect NICHOLAS 11. has an advan- tage over some of his predecessors on the Russian throne,and that is in regard to education. By the , time he was twenty-one he could, it is said, speak fluently in four languages—Russian, German, French and English. His course of reading has included the works of Shakespeare, Scott, Dickens and Robert Louis Stevenson, and in French his favourite authors are Victor Hugo and Lamartine. In his days of greater freedom and less responsi- bility he more than once visitedlthis country. He was present at the marriage of the DUKE of YORK, and—strange experience for a Russian ruler—he has listened from the Distinguished Strangers' Gallery of the House of Commons to the out- spoken debates of the elected representatives of the people. Ho has too seen more of the world than most other Russian princes. In 1891, he travelled to India, and visited China and Japan. In this last-named country he narrowly escaped assassination. As he was passing through one of the narrow streets a native policeman suddenly struck at him with a sword and inflicted a wound on the head, and if it had not been for the prompt interposition of Prince GEORGE of Greece the CZAR might not have been the central figure at the present celebrations in Moscow. With reference to his political leanings, it is said that the CZAR was at one time strongly in favour of an understanding with England. Whether he still holds the same opinions is not known, though after certain recent events byth in the Turkey and in the Further East, it may be doubted whether he is quite so well dis- posed towards this country as he used to be.

TIM TIMBER TBADTC ON THE CANAL. Where is every probability that the traffic on the Ship Canal in Runcorn and the district will be somewhat brisk during the next few weeks. There are 13 or 14 vessels expected to bring large cargoes of timber as far as Runcorn layt y to be transhipped into barges for con• veyance to Manchester. The first of the fleet arrived at Saltport on Thursday with a cargo of 1,800 tons of logs for various merchants in Lancashire. It is expected that the wharves whereby the accommodation at the lavby has been extended, will be used for the first time since their construction by the timber vessels which are expected so soon to reach this neighbourhood. This Increased traffic,it is to be hoped, will have the effect of providing employment for a number of unemployed workmen.

RUNCORN AND 1.7-.[II)NES.

MILL BROW WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL, RUNCORN. The above school held the anniversary services on Sunday last in the'Elalton Road Church. The Rev. George Bask of Manchester, formerly a minister in the circuit, preached at both services. Special music was rendered by the scholars, asiisted by the choir. The collections for the day totalled £23, which is an advance of £2 ftn the previous year. RUNCORN AND DISTRICT TEACHERS' ASSOCIATION.

On Friday evening„ under the auspices ot the above association a public meeting will be held in the Bethesda Lecture Hall, Runcorn. The meeting is called to con- sider some of the details of the Education Bill now be- fore Parliament. Mr. J. C. Horobin, M.A., will deliver an address. Managers of schools, teachers and others interested in the question of education, are cordially invited.

BETHESDA CONGREGATIONAL SUNDAY SCHOOL, RUNCORN.

On Sunday the anniversary sermons in connection with the above Sunday school ware preached in the Bethesda Church, by the Rev. A. H. Cullen of Manches- ter. In the afternoon the Rev.o de P. Gliddon of Halton, gave an instructive address on" Five stones o.tof a brook." The attendance throughout was very good,and the collections amounted to £8 ss. id. a trifle less than the amount taken last year. MERSEY MISSION TRIP, RUNCORN....

On Whit-Tuesday this annual excursion will be held as usual. As in recent years it has been an unqualified success, there is every reason to believe it will be largely patronised. Every accommodation has been provided, and the" Snowdrop" being a New Brighton boat, the tare charged (Is. 61), will include all pier dues at New Brighton. It is expected that the excursion will be ac- compani..d by the Volunteer band. Further particulars will be found in an advertisement.

DRUNK AT RUNCORN.

On Thursday before Messrs. F. J. Norman and J. W. Lightburn at the Runcorn Police Court, Henry Peaketh Mather, was charged with being drunk and disorderly in High-street on the previous day, from the evidence of P.C. Booth it was proved that prisoner was found drund, and creating a disturbance in High-street in the evening. The officer who had previously warned him for begging in Ir well-lane, locked him up. Defendant was ordered either to pay 20s. and ss. 4d. costs or in default compulsory retirement for 21 days.

SALE OF PROPERTY AT RUNCORN. On Monday evening Mr. Luke Holmes offered for sale at the South Bank Hotel several lots of property situ- ated in Mersey-3t., Granville-at., Cooper st., Union-st., Bentinck-st., and Hope-st., Runcorn. The dwelling- house, 8, Cooper-st., with garden land in the rear, Bub- ject to a ground rent of £2 133. per annum, was pur- chased by Mr. Dugdala of Alvanley; for £lO5. The freehold dwelling-house at 52, Union-st., occupied by Mr. Samuel Fletcher, was sold for £165 to Mr. Parting" ton. The rest of the lots when put up did not reach the reserve, and were accordingly withdrawn. Air. F. A. Lake was the solicitor for the matter. ACCIDENT AT THE OLD QUAY RUNCORN. On Saturday morning about seven o'clock, Thomas Cox of Halton, was seriously injured, while assisting to load the steamer" Homewood," lying in the Ship Can with brickettes. While on the stage it is believed he wa Feized with giddiness foT he fell off into the vessel's hold, a depth of more than a dozen feet. Dr. McDougall was at once sent for, and on examination found toe man suffering from severe laceration on the left side of the face, compound fracture of the jaw in several places, fracture of two or three ribs and serious shock to the system. On being taken home he was placed under the care of Dr. Carruthers and under that gentleman's ad- vice was taken to the Royal Infirmary Liverpool on Sat- urday night.

RUNCORN CTCLE CLUB. The church parade, which is now an annual event in connection with the above club,took place on Saturday. The members and a number of unattached wheelmen met at the headquarters, Bridze-street,about two o'clock and a start was made for Dutton where the club was received by the Vicar, the Rev. J. Holden. The machines were left on the vicarage lawn, after which the cyclists numbering about. 60 and including several ladies attended a spceial service. The service was conducted by the vicar, the Rev. J. Holden, who preached an appropriate ad lress. A collection was taken on behalf of the Warrington Infirmary.—The Saturday section of the club made Sale Botanical Gardens the headquarters for the week end run, and a pleasant afternoon was spent. The, Wednesday fixture for the club run should have been Cranage but the weather was so unfavourable that very few of the members turned out.

RUNCORN RURAL COUNCIL AND SHIP CANAL BILL

A special meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Coun- cil was held on Monday afternoon at the Workhouse, Dutton, for the signing and sealing of a petition to the House of Lords against the proposals of the Ship Canal Bill to close the tidal openings. The chair was occupied 1 by Mr. J. Mclntyre, J. P. The Clerk (Mr. A3hton) read the petition, which was a very lengthy one. It clearly indicated the townships,the area, and the rateable value of the Council's district, as well as the length of frontage on the canal ;.showing that sewage works had been con- structed in Appleton, Frodsham, Frodsham Lordship and Weston;: explained that by the Act of 1885 the Canal Company undertook not to interfere with the sewers until they had provided fresh outfalls ; pointed out that they had failed to d,o that, and proceedings were insti- tuted for an injunction to restrain them, which was granted, and it was made a cocdition precedent that they should enter into the agreement of 1891 ; that the Company had not constructed all the works under the agreement ; and the memorialists urged that the tidal openings being closed would interfere with the sewers. They claimed to have the Bill dismissed, but if their Lordships considered the preamble proved, to have cer- tain clauses inserted for the full protection of the Coun- cil's district.—On the motion of Mr. Heywood, seconded by Mr. Aaron, it was unanimously resolved to affix the seal of the Board to the petition, which was accordingly done, and the same afternoon it was forwarded to London, Tuesday being the last day for receiving • memorials.

RUNCORN AND THE CANAL BILL.

On Wedneecia.;,-a meeting of the Parliamentary Corn- mittee of the Runcorn District Council took place at the Town Hall. Runcorn. The Ship Canal Bill as it is to be presented to the House of Lords was fully considered and it was decided,seeing the amendments made by the House of Commons,to recommend that no further steps be taken in oppositioa to the Bill.—At the same meet- ing it was decided to petition against the Bill confirm- ing certain provisional orders made by the Local Govern- ment Board in connection with the Port Sanitary Auth- ority. The grounds for the petition were based on tae fact that it would be unjust to throw upon a small district a burden far in excess of thdt placed on districts which are rapidly increasing and will have control over and obtain the chief benefits to be derived from the authority. The injustice was shown by a comparison of the amount in the wiled on the rates which the pro- posel scheme would inflict. If the only expenditure reached £l,BOO the Rural Council would have to levy a rate of nearly Is. 21. in the 2, while Manchester would only require 1-18 and Salford 1-5 in the 2 for their share. But as unjust as this is it would eventually besome more so, for as traffic was making to the Man- chester end the benefit to Runcorn deminished. The memorial contended that if Runcorn had to contribute to the anthority, other districta such as Widnes, War- rington, Ellesmere Port, Irla.m and Manchester should also pay a share.

TRH APPROACH OF WHITSUNTID3 AT RUNCORN. The coming of Whitsuntide at Ruocorn is perhaps, to the juvenile portion of the community, one of the most important events of the year. The mere fact of the period being the Whitsun season does not provide any theme for interest to most of the children,but its atten- dant features, the gala day en Monday, the travelling on Tuesday, and the cessation of school for the week, make it a time to be looked forward to with eager anti- cipation. Most of the organisations and schools taking part in the procession are now busy making prepara- tions for the event which will follow very much on the lines laid down in former years. For Tuesday there are any amount of attra;•tions, chief of which will be the Seamen's Mission trip. Mr. Shaw has again fixed this pleasant outing for Tuesday, and has chartered a steamer to take his patrons down the river to Liverpool and New Brighton. The railway companies are also putting forth announcements bearing upon the different attractions of both local and distant pleasure resorts. Looking at the bills anyone will see thi.t for the next week travelling will be cheap to nearly all available pleasure grounds in this part of England, while longer period excursions can be made to as far as scotland on the one side, and London ,on th, other. Business in Runcorn for two or three days will be at a stand still. As will be seen from an announcement in our advertising columns the Manchester Ship Canal Cmnpany give netice of their in- tention to suspend work at Runcorn Docks, Bridgewater Wharves, and Saltport on Monday and Tuesday. The schools in the district are also closing this week end, for the Whit-week,so the the holidays as far as the children are concerned,will not be trammelled with the thoughts of education either under the new or old code. Mcst of the business houses and firms in the Gown will be closed on Monday and Tuesday.

HONOUR FOR A RUNCORN COUNIY

COUNCILLOR.

On Thursday afternoon news was received in Runcorn that Mr. P. Speakman,a Runcorn represen- tative on the Cheshire County Council,had been se- lected to fill an aldermanic vacancy on the Council. Mr. Speakman is well known in Runcorn, and the honour shown him has been well deserved. Not only has he been a member of the County Cum:mil since that august body was formed, but he is also on the Commission of Peace, and officiates as a magistrate on the Bench. He is also a mem- ber of the Runcorn Urban Pistrict Council. As Mr. Speakman is a supporter of the Liberal cause the news was received with pleasure by the Liberals of the town who the same evening began to cast about for a Liberal candidate for the coming election to fill the vacancy caused by Mr. Speak man's promotion to the aldermanic seat. A meet- ing was called the same evening (Thursday) in the Primitive Methodist schoolroom and a number of active Liberals attended, including Mtssrs. Basnett, S. Crosby, J. Greenhalgh, B. Kniveton, S. Walton, J. W. Bailey, W. Hoxv.,orth, J. Wilson. W. Mundy, P. Leathwood. Wicktball and others. Mr. Speakman occupied the chair, after congratulatory speeches had been made by several gentlemen on the distinguished honour conferred upon the chair, the meeting proceeded to select a candidate to stand in the coming election to represent the Liberal in- terests. On the motion of Mr. J. Bazley, seconded by Mr. Hoxworth, and supported by Messrs. .Knive- ton, Dean. and Posnett, it was resolved to ask Mr. A. R. Norman, to allow himself to be nominated. Mr. Norman accepted the invitation and thanked the meeting for the confidence it had reposed in him.—There is every reason to believe that a con- test for the seat will be the result, and already ramour is busy with the names of several promin- ent Conservates likely to oppose Mr. Norman. As the election will very quickly follow—probably next Saturday but one—something definite will have to be speedily settled.

EXTRAORDINARY DEATH OF A RUNCORN CHILD.

SUPPOSED FATAL FALL FROM A SWING.

Mr. Worsley held an inquest at the Police Station, Runcorn,on Tuesday,on the body of Lawrence Lenghna, who died on Saturday last at 19, Friar-st. Runcorn. Annie Lenghna, wife af James Lenghna, chemical labourer, said the deceased was her child and was three years Old. About a month ago her child fell off a swing between two walls. Her son was brought home crying, , and another child told her that—" Cissie Donovan haul knocked him off the swing." He was not well afterwards and she had to call a doctor who attended him up to the time of his death.—Dr. Delaney, said he was called to see the deceased and attended him up to the time of death. The child complained of pains in the region of the spine. The deceased died on Saturday night and the cause of death was meningitis. It was possible that meningitis might have occurred without the fall but a severe fall might have set it up. He would say that death was from meningitis probably occasioned by the fall. There was a slight contusion of the forehead. —Catherine Donovan, a girl of about 10 years, living in Friar-st.' said she remembered deceased falling from the swing. There was a lot of children playing on the swing and deceased in jumping off it fell. He said he was not hurt, and he did not tumble on his head.—A verdict in accordance with the medical testimony was returned.

Music.—" The Ot phan's Prayer" is the title of a new and tenderly pathetic ballad, the words of which are by Mr. L. B. Butler,and the music by Mons. Gustave Chan- doir. The song is rendered at the St. James' Hall, Lon- don, by Master Buffery, and as a loncly boy's supplica- tion to his departed mother it is extremely touchirw,and meets with the greatest possible success. Mr.Butter,the author, it may be mentioned, is secretary to Messrs. Moore and Burgess.

THE DROWNING OF A SEAMAN AT

ItIINCOItN

INQUEST.

On Tuesday morning at the Police Statior,Mr.Worsley held an itquest on the body of Donald Sanderson, an able bodiea seaman, aged 34, who was drowned in the Weston Canal on Saturday night. Donald Tabish, an able-bodied sailor on board the Kenilworth, said he had known Donald Sanderson 7 or 8 weeks since he had been with the vessel. Deceased was a seaman. On Saturday the Kenilworth was lying at Weston Point and he and deceased came to Runcorn. They left the ship about 4 30,and made some purchases in Runcorn. It was close to eleven o'clock when they left the " Duke of Wellington "for the ship. When they got on board they turned back for a cap deceased had missed. Deceased had had more drink than he had but they were both under the influence of drink. They went back and passed the " Duke of Wellington." They could not find the cap so returned towards the ship. They were going down to the canal making for the bank and deceased who was about ten yards before him fell into the canal. He lay down on the bank and saw deceased come up. He was about three yards from him and he went down and never came up again. He could not swim but called out and a man came. He told the man that he had lost his mate down there (pointing to the canal) and the man looked down and said there was no man there. Some other men came along and he told them and one offered to dive but he teld the man that deceased was dead. He then went on board and told the captain who said he could not do anything."—William Johnson, landlord of the Duke of Wellington public-house, said he saw deceased and the last witness at his house. They came in about half-past eight. They stayed about five or ten minutes. Deceased was slightly under the influ- ence of drink though not drunk, while the previous wit- ness seemed sober. Deceased came out with very bad language, and he persuaded them to go out. P.C. Gregory said he was present when the body was recovered. It was fully dressed except the hat. He examined the boiy and there were no marks of violence. He had in his pockets Zs. 6d., a knife, a box of matches, and some receipts. From the ship's papers he found deceased's age was 34. He was found in the Weston casual near the Arnold Dock. For anyone not knowing the way very well it was a dangerous path. In answer to the Coroner: The road could be protected.—Continu- ing, a man named James Porter made a statement to him that he (Porter) was going to Runcorn about 12.30 when he met a man, and he told him something which he could not understand as he was that drunk he could not speak. Porter said he told the man to go home and mind he did not go down. On the Sunday when he heard of the man being found in the canal. Porter thought that the incident had something to do with it. Porter had since sailed. Witness also saw the captain of the Kenilworth who said that Tabish did not tell him of the accident until Sunday morning about eight o'clock. He then gave information to the police. The Coroner in putting the case to the jury, said the explanation of the matter was that the two men were hopelessly intoxicated. The story told by Tabish was probably true in substance, but he was cot in a position ta remember the times and places connected with the occurrence. They must have both being hopelessly in- toxicated, and the wonder was that the other man had not fallen in. It had been said that the road was dan- gerous. He did not know if there was anyone present in connection with any authority governing the road, but if so attention might be drawn to the fact that the road was dangerous, and it would be better if it was made safe —A Juror: The road is a private one.—The Coroner: In that case we can only send a recommenda- tion. A verdict of "Found drowned" was returned, and the foreman stated that he would see the owner of the road, and place the recommendation of the jury before him. Donald Sanderson, the seaman who was unfortunately drowned on Saturday, leaves a wife and four children, and the widow wrote to the superintendent of police stating that she was unable to come to Runcorn and had no means to bury her husband. Through the representa- tions of Mr. Shaw ef the Seamen's Missionary, the body was buried in the sailors' grave. At the Cemetery on Thursday Mr. Shaw was met by Mr. W. Byfield, Mr. T. Shaw, and a number of sailors who attended the funeral.

DRUNK AND ASSAULTING THE POLICE

AT RUNCORN.

A MELEE IN DUKE-STREET.

At the Petty Sessions held at Runcorn on Monday morning. William Walker was charged with being drunk and aisorderly in Duke-at.. on Saturday night, with assaulting P.C. Wylie at the same time and place. and with damaging a policeman's uniform to the extent of 153.—P.C. Wylie said he was on duty on Saturday night about 11.30 with Inspectornicks, Sergeant Lee, and P.C. Lakin. Their attention was directed to Duke- St., where they proceeded and found the street in an up- roar. There was a general disturbance in which Walker was the ringleader. Walker, a 11,n asked refused to give his name, and the Inspector told witness to take the prisoner down to the police station. Prisoner refused to go and became very abusive, and kicked him several times about the legs. Sergeant Lee assisted him, and prisoner becoming quiet he was left to take him on to the police station. On the way prisoner was again very violent and kicked him about the legs and tore his trousers. He had to handcuff prisoner before he could get him down. Inspector Hicks said that while he was in Duke-street thero was a most disorderly and rough crowd, such as he had not seen in Runcorn for at least 12 months. The prisoner and two other men who were also in custody were fighting and he ordered P.C. Wylie to take him to the Police Station. Prisoner was both impudent and abusive. He saw him assault the constable. If there had been less than four constables there, one of them would have been seriously injured in the melee.—Prisoner was fined 53. and 63.4 d. costs for being drunk, 103. and 53. costs for the assault and 15s. damage to the uniforn. George Handles was also charged with being drunk and disorderly in Duke-3treet on Saturday night and also with assaulting P.C. Lakin.—P.C. Lakin,who stated the case, said that during the course of the disturbance he was helping P.C. Wylie when prisoner who WAS standing at the back struck him a blow under the ear. He at once arrested the defendant who was drunk.—lnspector Hicks said that while Constable Lakin was assisting Wylie he saw the defendant deliberately strike Lakin behind the ear.—Fined ss. and 63. 4d. costs for druaken- ness and 103. and 17s. costs for the assault. Arising out of the same cases was a charge of drunkenness while in Duke-street against Nicholas Johnson.—P.C. Wylie stated that defendant was one of the ringleaders but quietened down when he saw the police.—A fine of 53. and costs amounting in all to us. 4d. was imposed.

FRODSBAIS.

TEACHERS' PICNIC. —On Saturday afternoon the teachers connected with OyArton Sunday school, ac- companied by the Revs. W. T. Dickenson and E. A. Simms, left Frodsham by the 1.38 train for Chester whence they were conveyed per waggonette to Hawar- den Castle. After spending a couple of hours in and about the castle and grounds, a substantial tea was pro- vided at the Glynne Arms. The rest of the afternoon was spent in visiting the church and other points of in- terest in the locality, and the pleasant outing was thoroughly enjoyed, Frodsham being reached about nine o'clock. The entire expense of the excursion was borne by the Misses Wright, who take a deep interest in the working of the church Sunday school.

FAULESTOWN BAPTIST CHURCH. The special anniversary services in aid of the Sunday School connected with the above church were held on Sunday. At each service there were large congregations, and appropriate discourses were preached morning and evening by the Rev. G. W. Brooker, of Leigh, special hymns being sung by the choir. In the afternoon a sacred recital was given by the choir and the Earlestown Orchestral Society, the latter under the leadership of Mr. Ashley. EARLESTOPirN COLLIER.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, James Downs,collier, Legh-street, Earlestown, claimed from the Collins Green Colliery Company £2 damages for dismissal on the 13th March last in lieu of notice. The company made a counter-claim for £4 43. for neglect of work at various times previously extending to January.—Mr. Riley, solicitor, St. Helens, appeared in support of the claim on behalf of the St. Helens Branch of the Miners Federation. Mr. Percy Davies, solicitor, represented the defendant

company. James Downs stated that he had been employed as a collier by the Collins Green Colliery Company for a number of years. When he was engaged he signed an agreement with the company. On the 13th of March he had finished_ a " place " in the mine. The under-

manager told witness that there were no more empty places for him and witness and his mates must fetch their tools out of the pit. They did so. Witness went to the colliery on the following day about three o'clock in the afternoon, and saw John Thomas Mehers, one of the underground foremen, and asked him it he could find him (witness) a place. Mehers said he hadn't time to look round then, but told him to ccme again. Witness did so, and Mehers then told him that if he weuld come again on the following Friday he would find him a place. Witness went but Mehers then said that Mr. Cunliffe had put on another . .

set of men in the place where he (Mehers) was thinking of setting witness and others to work. Witness did not go to the colliery again but he afterwards received a message from the miners agent (Mr. Glover) stating that Mr. Jackson, the manager of the Collins Green Colliery wanted to see hina. Mr. Jackson asked witness about Mr. Glover's letter which claimed wages for wit- ness in lieu of notice of dismissal. Mr. Jackson asked him whether it would not be better to put his money in the Post Office instead of in the Miners' Union. Wit- ness said it might be but he did not think so. He also I told Mr. Jackson that h^ had to let Mr. GI )ver know I what Mr. Jackson had said. Mr. Jackson then told him that he could tell Mr. Glover to proceed with the case and he would defend it. Witness had not been able to get work since he was told,to bring his tools out on the 13th March last. He never received seven days' notice. —ln answer to questions by Mr. Davies witness said he was paid by the ton. He did not know he would finish the place or contract on which he had entered by a certain time. He could not tell how long the place would last. The under-manager did not tell him seven days before he was told to bring his tools out that the company would not be able to keep him on when the place was finished. He did not know his employment would cease. He supposed the Colliery Company would find him another place as they had done previously or give him a week's notice. Witness did complain to the manager when free h men were engaged before himself or his brother. He was ill when he stayed away from his work. He had earned as much as 94. a day at the colliery. He had not refused to work for the company. He admitted that he told Mr. Jackson in the interview he had with him that he (witness) was not making the claim against the company. He did not know the Federation was claiming £2 damages.—On being re-examined by Mr. Riley, witness said that the. com- pany had changed his place in the colliery about twice. William Downs, 61, Viaduct-st., Earlestown, said he had worked as drawer for his brother (the plaintiff), at the Collins Green Colliery Company for about eight years. He corroborated the plaintiff's statement as to lbeing told to bring his tools out. He considered that as receiving the "sack." Witness did not belong to the Miners' Federation. rie had since found work at another colliery. For the defence,Mr. Davies said the greater portion of the evidence was untrue. The plaintiff and others were engaged in a certain place and were to remove the coal at a certain price per ton. The work had nothing to do with the engagement contract. When they had cleared the place their work was finished and the plain- tiff and those working with him knew it. Some time before they had removed all the coal at that place, the plaintiff was told by the manager that he would soon be finished. The plaintiff knew that he would then be out of work. He was a man who had not attended to his work regularly. Sometimes he only worked one day a week, and he was told that when he had finished the piece on which he was engaged the company would not have him any longer. When he left he never knew that he had any claim against the company or he would have made it. The Miners Federation had instituted those proceedings on statements which had been made behind the back of the colliery company. The plaintiff after he had taken his tools away wit offered a Job but he refused to have it because he would have to work with two other men. They seemed to be approaching an age when workmen could dictate to a colliery com- pany where they were going to work, when they could work as they liked, and do as they liked. Andrew Jackson,manager of the Collins Green Coiliery Company, said the plaintiff was engaged in get- ting coal. He was paid is. 4d. a ton, 30 per cent, on that. and 4d. A ton for drawing according to the arrangement arrived at. The company employed 325 workmen. The plaintiff was is very irregular worker. If he liked to have work he could have it. He did not stop away always on account of illness. Be received the letter produced from Mr. Glover, miner's agent. The plaintiff told him that it was not his claim and that he knew nothing at all about it. He told plain- tiff that he would find him a place if he would work.—ln answer to Mr. Riley,witnese said the company had never claimed from a workman who had left without giving notice. The plaintiff was employed until his place was finished. The plaintiff had notice given to him. Wit- ness considered that the contract was at an end. Colliery owners were in the hands of the miners. He thought it was wrong for the Federation to bring them to the court time after time. When they wanted a man to leave, he admitted, they generally gave him notice. John Thomas Mehers, underlooker, and residing at Railway View, Collins Green, said the plaintiff would know when he had finished his place. It was not cus- tomary to give a man who was working as the plaintiff, any notice for him to firish. On the ih March, wit- ness offered the plaintiff a place with two men named Hinglish and another named Ogden, but he would not work at it, stating that he wanted a place for himself and brother. In cross-examination, witness said he knew that the contract entered into stipulated that seven days notice must be taken and given, but he had never known it to be enforced. Henry Williams, fireman at the colliery and living at Albert Terrace. Collins Green. said he was present when Mr. Mehers offered the plaintiff a place. The plaintiff re- fused to have it, and also declined a job witness offered him of" driving a brow." Workmen had been able to leave without giving notice. The plaintiff had been receiving 14s. a week from the Federation during the time he was out of work. James Cunliffe, under-manager, said he had worked for the company for about seven years. Witness told plaintiff about a week previous to the 13th March that when he had finished his place there was no other for him for the present. He told the plaintiff to remove his tools on the 13th March. Mr. Riley urged on behalf of the plaintiff that where there was a written contract between two people it was absolutely binding. The contract remained in force so long as the workman continued to be employed at the colliery. On the 13th March, the plaintiff was a work- man tbere.and was subject to the obligations of the con- tract which he had entered into with the company. It was therefore incumbent tapon the company to carry out their part of the agreement. The men were entitled to reasonable notice before being dism;ssed, and it was unreasonable to expect them to hang about the colliery on the off-chance of work being found for them. No evidence had been offered with reference to the counter- claim. Mr. Davies said the company withdrew the' counter- claim. His Flonsur said the difficulty arose front the lax practises of the colliery company. He did not mean that there was any culpable negligence or that there was any improper relations between employers and employed. On the contrary, from the evidence given, he believed the colliery company were prepared to deal in a friendly way with their men. But the easy-going way of dealing with the men did not alter the letter of the contract which existed between the company and the men. On their own printed copy of the rules which was given to each man on signing his agreement there was a stipula- tion that there should be an insistence of seven days notice on either side. It was for an indefinite period and whenever a workman left or was discharged there was to be seven days notice. It had been urged that this contract did not apply in the present case because the plaintiff undertook to do a cer- tain piece of work for which he was paid ' by piece-work. He thought that Mr. Jackson's evidence showed clearly that the plaintiff was a collier and not a contractor. The plaintiff only did what other miners had to do. When the plaintiff took his tools from the colliery the relation between the Company and the plaintiff ceased, and by the contract he was entitled to seven days' notice. Be was therefore justified in claim- ing the damages. He would give a verdict for the plaintiff, allowing 365. damages and costs. The coun- ter claim was dismissed.

ILLITERATE VOTERS

A return in reply to Mr. R. C. Webster on the 28th February last, and just issued, shows the num- ber of persons who voted as illiterates at the general election of 1895. With this are given for comparison, the figures of 1892. In the Newton Division 9,212 votes were polled ; of these 78 were illiterates against 8,532 and 122 respectively in 1892; Widnes. 7,429 and 73 against 7,527 and 172; St. Helens, 8,791 and 341 against 8.457 and 562 Warrington, 7,327 and 188 against 7,101 and 288.

THE COLD WEATHER AND THE CROPS.

By the very keen frost on Wednesday night consirl r- able mischief was done in the orchards, gardens, and pQtato fields at many places in Lancashire and Cheshire. Apple and plum trees have suffered to a very large ex- tent, anti great damage has been caused amongst the early pltato crops. Frost after rain has been very much feared by growers. Owing to the frost many hundreds of acres of early potatoes have been completely des- troyed in South Westmoreland. This calamity has been more particularly felt in the missland districts, where a great revenue is annually expected from this early crop.

The prisoners Milsom and Fowler were convicted ca Thursday of the murder of Mr. Smith at Muswell Hill. Whilst the jury were considering their verdict Fowler made a ferocious attack on Miliom, and a terrible scene tollowed. The prisoner is a very powerful man, and he fought desperately in the dock with the policemen, who evevtually overpowered him. Mr. Justice Hawkins passed sentence of death on thr, prisoners,and told them there was no hope af a repri-. Fowler was led away from the dock whilst protestinz that he was the author of a serious crime at Lewisham for which two men ars 'now in custody.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

MOUE DDIORDEULY CONDUCT IN THE PLUIC.-EXEISPARLY

PENALTY.

On Monday morning, at the Warringtoi. Borough Court, Benjamin Timmins, 1, Collins-st., and Anthony Backhouse, 52, Priestley-st., were charged with being guilty of disorderly behaviour in Bank Park on May llth.—Frederick Scarisbrick, assistant gardener at the Park, said that he was crossing the recreation ground on May 11th when he noticed the defendants, They were behaving in a disorderly manner.—P.C. Cooper said he accompanied Mr. Ashton, the parkkeeper, to the piaci named on tbe day in question. He there saw defendants acting disorderly. He took them into one of the green- houses and there taxed them with the offence, which they admitted. They were taken before the Town Clerk and reported.—The Chief Constable pressed for the ulti- mate penalty in both cases, urging upon the Bench the necessity of keeping the Park "a clean place."—Both defendants were fined 40s. and costs (z 2 63. in all). THE POLICE.

On Tuesday before Mr. E. J. Guest (in the chair),and Mr. R. Garnett. James Donlan, 5 Brick-street, Warring- ton,and Joseph Ellison .11 Dial-street, Warrington,young men,were charged with breaking and entering the shop of Charles Paikin, 3 Dial-street, between three o'clock on Sunday and ten o'clock on Monday morning, and steal- ing a jacket value 103. 81 his property.—The Chief ConstAle said that Mr. Paikin last saw his shop secure about three o'clock on Sunday afternoon, when he left the premises. He returned about 11 o'clock on Monday morning and then found that the premises had been en- tered by thieves. They had scaled a wall, entered the yard,and attempted to enter by a lattice window. They had not succeeded here and a large square of glass was broken out of the top half of another window and the catch put back. A number of articles were disturbed but the only thing which was found to be missing was a new Icoat value 103. 81. Inspector Cox, wbo visited the scene of the theft, found on one of the window panes distinct marks of the knee of a pair of cor- duroy trousers. On the gate was the mark of a clog. On apprehension Ellison was found to be wearing trousers and clogs corresponding to the marks. On Monday evening Mary Leslie, general dealer, Mersey-street, was in her shop when Donlan entered wearing the missing coat. He wanted her to buy it for ss. and another coat but she refused, as she had had a description of the coat served her by the police. Just at that moment Inspecter Cox came up and seeing the defendants outside the shop took them into custody, and the coat was left in the shop. Ellison was wearing two coats, Donlan's and his own.—Charles Paikin, a Jew, general dealer of 2, Dial-street, and resid- ing in Lovely-lane, corroborated. Mrs. Leslie and Inspector Cox also supported the evidence of the Chief- Constable, the latter observing that when he came down Mersey-street on Monday night his attention was drawn to the defendants by their standing outside Mrs. Leslie's shop and Donlan being without coat and Ellison having two on.—ln answer to the charge Dlnlan said " As there is a God in heaven I did not know that coat wtis stolen." Ellison made no statement and both prisoners were committed to the next Qearter Sessions at Liver- pool for trial.

BURTONWOOD SCHOOL BOARD.

A meeting of this Board was held in the class- room of the Board Schools, when there were pre- sent—Messrs. Andrew Jackson (in the chair), Wil- liam Pairclough, Thomas Norcott, H. Gawthorpe (schoolmaster), and William IVebster (clerk). The Clerk reported that, on hearing of the death of the late Mr. James Warburton (who had been a member of the Board since the 6th April, 1882, and was thus the oldest member of the Board, and had been chairman since 20th April, 1882), he had consulted with the Vice-Chairman, and by his directions closed the schools on Tuesday and postponed the Board meeting from Tuesday to that evening. It was resolved that such action be approved.—A let- ter was read from the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, stating that he truly regretted their Chairman's death. The vote of condolence had his whole sympathy.—Mr. Pairclough moved, and Mr. Norcott seconded the following resolution :—" That this Board desires to place on record their appreciation of the services rendered to the inhabitants of Burtonwood by the late Chairman, Mr. James Warburton, and to ex- press to his widow and family their deep sympathy with them in their sad bereavement; and that the Clerk be directed to forward a copy of this resolu- tion to Mrs. Warburton." The motion was carried. The attendance returns were as follows :—St. Paul's of the Cross R.C., mixed,on register 108,aver- age 93, or 86 per cent. ; infantv, 87, 65, and 74.7 reg.. pectively: Board School, mixed 138,1124, and 81 .4 respectively ; infants, 59,43, and 73 respectively. The low infants' average is due to sickness. It was resolved to hold a special meeting of the Board for the purpose of filling the vacancy caused by the death of Mr. James Warburton.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

CRICKET.

When the Warrington team journeyed to Bootle on Saturday to play the Bootle first team, they did not expect to be placed on a field which would not do credit to a junior club. But such was the case, for there was a most ugly patch close to the pitch which made accurate play impossible. It may be urged that it was the came for both sides, but that is not so, for the hotnesters are well acquainted with it, and their fast bowler—Airey, manipulated the ball in such a manner as to make the defects of the field of advantage to his side. He " humped " the ball in such a fashion as to give almost every • Warrington batsman some nasty knocks. Mr. S. M. Crosfield was the first victim,and he was closely followed by Thewlis, who received such a severe blow on the temples as to compel him to retire. Other Warrington batsmen were hit about the body,and the captain, Mr. S. M. Crosfield, instructed the plaoers to run no risks in keeping up their wickets. The result was that they were all sent to the right about for the small total of 42, as against 92 by their opponents.

Connor again allowed Nis skill with the ball, and although he did not bowl from the same end at which Airay was able to do so much mischief, yet he catrieri all before him, his record being eight wickets for 33 runs. The two other Warrington bowlers,j. J. Crosfield and Scrimshaw, were punished rather severely, the former only taking one wicket for 26 runs, and the latter one for 23. Price,Clark, and Costain each reached double figures for the home team. Another double- figure item in connection with the Bootle score is that recorded for" Extras," numbering 10, which compares unfavourably with 0 allowed to Warrington.

With 92 runs required to equalise, Warrington started with S. M. Crosfield and J. Bibby. After eleven runs had been scored, the popular captain of the visiting team received a blow on the elbow, and. with his next . stroke he placed the ball into the hands of Costain. Bibby shortly after- wards was bowled by Price, and a further disaster befel Warrington when Thewlis was struck by the ball on the left temple and had to retire. Mreycontiriued to bowl dangerously and several of the visiting batsmen nar- rowly escaped serious injuries. Knocks on the legs and body were frequently oxperienced by the batsmen, and correct cricket became an impossibility. Two of the batsmen—Podmore and England—were given out by the Bootle umpire for leg before wicket, and these decisions were strongly questioned. At 42, the Warrinatonians were out, and thus concluded one of the least enjoy- able matches w hich it has been the lot of the Warrington team to engage in tor a long period.

The Warrington second eleven again won handsomely on Saturday when they met the Bootle second team on the Arpley enclosure. The home team was the first to go to the wickets but the start was not a very promis- ing one,foilr wickets falling for 67 runs. Of this num- beroldamson, the home captain, compiled 34. But the stand of the afternoon was yet to be made. Rushworth joined Duckworth and before a separation was made,the partnership had realised 84 runs. Duckworth contri- buted a faultless 50 and Rushworth 34. Of the five re- maining batsmen,only one reached double figures, viz., H Ashton,who carried his bat out for lb. The innings closed for a grand total of 200 runs. The visitors man- aged to reach thfee figure, but fell short of the total by 72 runs. Their best batsman was Rogers who scored 40 not out.

===== 0002601_18960523_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
YACHTING.

The Warrington Sailing Club which has recently altered its title of the Warrington and Bank Quay Sailing Club to the present one, holds its annual cup race on Whit Monday next. In former years this race has always been held on the Saturday following Good Friday but this year it had to be delayed owing to there being no tide sufficient for the race on that date. A capital entry has been obtained and it is fully expected that all numbered on the card will face the sts.rters. It the list several boats new to this district will be noticed, includ- ing "May," a New Brighton crack. " Fairy " and "Explorer." Great things are expected from the first named. The race will commence at the South End of Thewlis' wall, Bank Quay, the first boat is expected to be in sight at 10.15 a.m. This is much earlier than usual, but from the great interest being shown I have no doubt a large number of people will witness the finish.

At night the members will hold a general meeting at the Volunteer Inn, when the cup will ba- handed to the successful captain and the prize money will be distri- buted. A supper will be provided. The Challenge Cup, which has been on view in the window of Messrs. Mawson and Co., Sankey-street, is of splenlid des.gn ani'. was supplied by Messrs. E. and A. Eustance in 1895. Mr. Walter Warburton, cwner of " Pink," is the present holder.

There is now quite a small fleet of Nactts in the river at IVarrington,and at the present the various crews may be seen working like s" troopers" each night to get all things trim for Monday.

The Sailing Club Committee have engaged the steam launck " Umbria" to be used by the officials on the race day.

The wielders of the willow representing the Warring- ton Grammer School "caught a tartar" on Saturday when they paid a visit ta Alexandra Park, Manchester, with the intention of defeating the Hume Grammar School there. A good game was played and for each side a single player passed 30 runs. For the victors a Dutchman possessing the euphonious name of Van der Veer, was not out for 33, while Pierpoint for Warrington managed to compile a good 39 before being bowled by Hobdey. The Hulme team altogether scored 114 against the visitors 78.

A Partington eleven entertained St. Elphins at Part- ington on Saturday and going in first were only able to put together 56 runs. Gresty finished them off in fine style, taking 6 wickets for an average of 2 runs per wicket. On the Saints taking the pitch an ommuus beginning was made, for Woods, R3binson and Taylor were converted from batsmen into spectators with only 3 runs between them. Duckworth, however, came to the rescue with 12 and other dozen coming from Maskell and no more" ducks" being made,they eventually won by 12 runs. This was a very near thing as 11 of tbe 12 were extras.

The "guardians of the peace" are not showing up very brilliantly as cricketers, it may be that the differ- ence in weight and shape between a cricket bat and a truncheon bothers them. Last Siturday they engaged the Moore team at Moore, and could only contrive to make 19 against 68 for the homesters. Five " ducks " were captured and the top score among the police was 3, though 6 runs were added to the total for extras. Millington, of Moore, scored just the same number of runs himself as the whole of the Police' team, viz., 13. This is however the first season for the Police and they have time enough to improve.

Two sets of Wesleyans were batting for supremacy at Penketh on Saturday when the Windsor Wesleyans went there and succeeded in defeating the homesters by 97 against 51. The fielding of the home side was so feeble as to be mainly the cause of defeat. More smartne-s than was displayed on Saturday will be needed if the Penketh Wesleyans are going to have a decent record to show at the end of the season.

The Whitecross eleven returned hcme vic- torious from Widnes on Saturday by a majority of 32 runs. Kenwright, Evans, and Gibbons were mainly responsible for this result by their good batting, and also by the sure bowling of the first named. None of the players for " the chemical town" reached double figures.

On Saturday afternoon the Cheshire Lines Engineers had a big job on hand when they visited Sutton to play the local team. A good attempt was made but the homesters proved too good for the Engineers by 81. runs to 50. Taylor contributed 29 for the winners,so that he may be regarded almost as the winner of the match. S. Walker made the best score for the C.L.E's. Harrison bowled excellently and raised the hopes of the visitors to a high pitch by taking 6 wickets for 24 iuns. Taylor, however, seemed to like the bowling and kept the ball traveiling. A somewhat singular thing, and moreover one that has occurred twice this season with local teams, is that the juniors of the same clubs played a match in which the result was the same, namely,a, majority of 31 runs for Sutton. Another coincidence about the same match was that in this case also a Taylor hatted first and won the match by compiling over 30 runs. The village oe Sutton ought to be fond of coincidences.

Buckley-street and St. Ann's tild a game on the ground of the latter on Saturday and scoring was slow on both sides. The homesters retired with 41 and the visitors were only able to meet this with 23.

THE EXAMINER SATURDAY, MAY 23, 1896.

EARLESTOWN DRUIDS.

On Saturday evening the Earlestown Lodge of the Order of Druids had a social evening, held chiefly for the purpose of infusing new life into the mem- bers. The proceedings included a substantial knife and fork tea at the lodge, the rrince of Wales llotel,provided by Mr. R. Lythgoe, to which there were about 40 present. After tea a convivial even- ing was spent, songs, &c., being contributed by members and friends. EARLESTOWN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

The 142nd quarterly meeting of this society was held in the Co-operative Hall, Earlestown, on Mon- day evening. Mr. D. Forrester presided, and there was a very large attendance of members. The re- port and balance sheet showed that the cash re- ceipts over the counter for goods sold was £4,061 its. 011., showing a decrease below last quarter of £2 13. lid. The nett profits available for dividend on purchases were £550 83. Bd., out of which it was proposed, to pay a dividend of 2s. 91. in the £ to members requiring £546 us. 3d.. and is. 4d. in the .E to non-members, requiring 143. 81, leaving a balance of £3 2s. 91. to be added to the contingent fund. During the quarter 15 new members had been admitted, and 9 struck off, leaving the mem- bership at 949, of whom 624 had brought in checks. The following were elected to serve on the Com- mittee of Management :—Messrs. T. Swift, D. Bar- croft, and W. Clare, the latter to serve three months in place of Mr. S. Barrett who has resigned. Mr J. Jones was re-elected stock-taker. It was decided to grant the same amount for the employes' picnic, as on previous occasions. It was decided to invite the Bolton District Council to hold their conference this year at Earlestown. The question of a testi• monial or retiring gratuity to the late manager,Mr. T. Hargreaves, who has been compelled to resign through ill-health was brought up,and after a brief discussion it was decided to present him with an illuminated address, and a purse of £5O, a special meeting to be called for the purpose of the presen- tation. The question of filling the vacancy caused by the resignation of the manager was intrbduced, but no decision was arrived at, and the meeting was adjourned for further consideration of the matter. The adjourned meeting was held on Thurs- day night, but there was only a moderate attend- ance of members. The question of arranging for the duties of the late manager was again brought forward, and after some discussion it was decided to continue as a temporary arrangement the appointment of Mr. H. Birks as manager, with Messrs. Parr and Swift to perform the secretarial work, until the revision of the rules is completed. The legality of the gratuity of £5O, voted to the manager was brought into question and the com- mittee were requested to lay the whole facts before the society's solicitor and obtain his opinion upon them.

MEDICAL AND SURGICAL AID SOCIETY, EARLEsTOWN.

The annual collection in aid of the funds of the above society, which was taken up at the Vulcan Foundry last week, amounted to a 125., which Mr. A. Peaks the secretary has acknowledged.

HORSE AND CATTLE FAIR AT NEWTON.

The May Fair for the sale of horses and cows was held at Newton on Monday. The cattle fair in the morning was successtul both in point, of numbers and quality. Prices also were high ranging for milking cows from £l7 to .V 23 heifers and stirks also bringing good figures. In the afternoon a large number of horses was offered for sale, but the quality was only moderate, and little business was done. The transactions in cattle were fairly numerous.

===== 0002601_18960523_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
DRUNKENNESS.

At the Earlestown Police Station on Monday morning, before Mr. C. B. F. Borron, John Cunning- ham, of Warrington, and John Flaherty, for being drunk in Earlestown on Saturday, were each fined 2s. 6d. and costs, and in default were sent to gaol or 7 days,—On Tuesday morning before Mr.Borron, William Pate, farmer, of Huston, near Chorley, was charged with being drunk in charge of a horse at Newton fair, the previous evening, and was fined 2s. 6d. and costs.—On Wednesday morning Peter Mclieown,of no fixed residence, for being drunk at Newton on Tuesday,was finad 2s.6d.and costs.and in default was sent to gaol for 7 days. GOOD IEMPLARY (EBENEZER LODGB). The Earlestown Ebentz-d. Lodge held its weekly session on Tuesday evening in the Co-operative Hail, Earle-street. Bro. T. Dean, C.T., presided. One candidate was admitted to membership. The L.D. Bro. W. J. Taylor read District Lodge circular, the next session to be held at Ormskirk on the 30th inst. The secretary, Bro. J. Taylor gave the report of No. 1 Convention held at Ashton on Saturday last. Bro. A. Francis, D.S.J.T., of St. Helens, gave an address, and thanks were tendered to him for his visit. Programme for evening " New officers to entertain." Sister Owens a song, Sister Woods a song, Bro. T. Dean a stump speech, after which the lodge closed. SERIOUS ACCIDENT TO A COLLIERY MANAGER.

On Monday, Mr. John Smallshaw, surface man- ager, at the Bold Colliery of the Collins Green Colliery Company, met with a serious accident, which it is feared will terminate fatally. Mr.Small- shaw was superintending the building of a new engine house, and was on a scaffold about 18 feet from the ground, when he slipped and fell heavily to the earth. He was removed unconscious to the Providence Hospital, St. Helens,where it was found that he had sustained injuries to the spine,and was completely paralysed. He lies in a precarious con- dition, and in order to ascertain the position and nature of his injuries the new photography was brought into use on Wednesday. GOOD TEMPLAR&

The weekly session of the Hope of Earlestown Lodge of Good Templars was held in the Co-opera- tive Hall on Monday evening. Bro. G. Clarke, C.T., presided, and there was a good attendance of mem- bers. The digest of business for the district lodge session was read by Bro. E. Newman, and represen- tatives instructed to attend. A report of the No.l Convention held the previous Saturday was read by Bro. E. Newman and adopted. The question of the annual pic-nic was left over tor a fortnight. The meeting was then declared open, the arrangements for the programme being in the hands of Bro. W. H. Crowther. Songs, recitations, •&c., were given by Sisters R. Warburton, M. Ord, A. Newman, J. Hill, Bros. Crowther, .1. Taylor, John Warburton and G. Clarke, and Bro. Newman, S.J.T., gave a short address. Bro. J. R. Muscrott presided at the piano. An enjoyable meeting was held. BURGLARS AT EARLESTOWN.

On Friday night about 11.45, Mr. Brammer, chief tailor to Mr. E. T. Evans, Market-street, returned to work to finish an order which was urgently required early on Saturday.and saw two men go suspiciously across the yard at the rear. He called Messrs. Owen and Parkinson in, and search was made. Nothing, however, was found to be missing, but Mr. Parkinson blew a whistle for the police, who also made a fruitless search. Mr. Owen saw a rag lying at the back door, and underneath discovered a jemmy. One of the mentad taken off his boots and left them on the stairs, but being suddenly dis- turbed it is surmised that he beat a 'quick retreat. Nothing more has been beard of the men, but it was subsequently found that a pair of boots were miss- ing from Mr. Hill's shop in Wargrave-road. SUDDEN DEATH Uie A BOLD FARMER AT HAILLBSTOWN.

On Monday afternoon Charles Whitfield, 69 years of age, a farmer, who lived at Bold, diei very sud- denly tit Earlestown railway station. He left borne in the morning accompanied by his son for the pur- pose of selling a horse at Newton fair. He disposed of the animal and left Newton by train about five o'clock for home. He had to change at Earlestown, and after walking from the curve to the station, he entered a carriage of the train which leaves Earles- town for St. Helens Junction at .5.25. As he was about to take his seat, he was observed to gasp,and immediately fell back. He was removed from the train, and was then found to be dead. The body was removed by the police to the Ram:'s Head Hotel and the coroner communicated with. Deceased had suffered fo- some time from heart diseas, and this is supposed to be the cause of death. '1 he doctor having certified that death was due to heart disase, the Coroner did not consider an inquest necessary.

VULCAN WESLEY.LV.-SUNDAY SCHOOL.

Special anniversary services in aid of the above school were held on Sunday afternoon and evening in the schoolroom. In the afternoon the service ot song entitled "Frances Ridley Havergal," was given by the choir, under the conductorship of Mr. C. Dickens, Master W. 11. Grundy presiding at the organ. The narrative was read by the Rev. W. Arrowsmith of St. Helens. There was a large audi- ence, and the service was creditably given, the principal vocal items being rendered by Misses Astall, Martin, Harrison and Mrs. Wadsworth. The Rev. W. Arrowswith remarked that it might be

interesting to those present to know that they had 160 scholars and 1e) teachers. During .the past year it had taken £l5 Bs. 10d. to inaintain the school,and he had been requested to ask them for £lO. He never asked anything unreasonable from acongreg- ation, and he did not think he was doing so on that

occasion. In the evening the rev, gentleman preached to n large congregation when special hymns were sung by the children and choir. The collections at both services amounted to £8 Is. 10d. VULCAN FOUNDRY READING ROOM.

On Saturday evening a concert in aid of the Reading Room connected with the employes of the Vulcan Foundry Co.' took place in the Vulcan schoolroom. Mr. W. Norman, works manager,pre- sided, and there was a crowded audience. Mr. No- rman apologised for the absence of Nir.Collingwood, who should have favoured the audience with a couple of vocal items, but he was away from home and could not be present. Referring to the objects of the concert, he said that up to about 18 months

, ago they had no place of amusement,no library and I no place to read in except their own homes,and the Company came forward and generously lent them the dining room free. They were sorry the reading room had not been patronised according to expec- tation, and he would regret very much to see the thing drop, but he hoped everyone present would that night become contianous members. There was no telling what they would come to if they received sufficient encouragement. The concert was then proceeded with, an excellent programme , of songs being given by Mr.G.Wright,,Mr.J. Nichols, Miss Williamson, Miss Conway, Mr. Roscoe (Whis- , ton) ; cornet solos by Mr. Baxter ; dulcimer solos by Mr. Taylor; recitation by Mr. G'radwell, and Mr. Vernon supplied the humour. Mr. W. Thompson Hand Mr. 11. Hunt accompanied. DARING ROBBERY AT EARLESTODT.

- On Thursday afternoon about four o'clock a rob- bery of a barefaced character was committed at the residence of Mr. Roberts, in King-st., Earlestown. Miss Roberts, wto is housekeeper for her father left the house for a few minutes to do some shop- ping. She locked all the house doors, but left the yard door open, her father, who is employed at Messrs. Evans and Co.'s signal cabin a few yards away, having gone up the line to oil some points. When he returned, he went to the house and found the kitchen window slightly open at which he was much surprised,as he found it had been forced open with a pick which was in the yard. On his daugh- ter returning a few minutes „afterwards, they went upstairs, and found the things in the Lack bedroom upset, and a search disclosed the fact that Mr. Roberts watch and gold chain and pendant, goLl ring, and a suit of clothes belocgMg to his son-in- law were missing. The matter was immediately reported to the poliee,and prompt search was made but up t 3 the time of writing no arrest has been made. Although the housl, is one of a long row,no- tody of a suspicious appearanpewas observed about, and there is no clue As to tile identity of rte thief or thieves,

WARRINGTON.

FUNERAL OF THE LATE MR. EDWARD BROCIELE- HURST, OF TRETTON.

The remains of the late Mr. Edward Brockle- hurst, of Road-side Farm, Stretton, were interred. in Stretton churchyard on Sunday afternoon. De- ceased was a :well-known villager, and during his lifetime held various offices connected with the church, in which he took a great interest as well as in the schools. For some time he had been in fail- ing health, but his death was not expected so soon. His medical attendant was Dr. Moberly. The funeral cortege left deceased's residence soon after four o'clock, and was met at the church by the Rev. Cannon Dodd, who conducted a very impressive service. The coffin was of English oak with brass mountings, and was covered with many beautiful wreaths sent by relatives and friends. Deceased was 45 years of age. The chief mourners were— Mrs. Brocklehurst, Messrs. Ernest, Herbert. and Roger Brooklehurst (sons), the Misses Emily and Mary Brocklehurst (daughters), Messrs. Joseph, Richard and James Brocklehurst (brothers), and Mr. and Mrs. Purslow, Mr. and Mrs. Frith, Mr. and Mrs. John Ditchfield, Mr. and Mrs. W. Darlington, Mr. and Mrs. T. Ditchfield, Mr. and Mrs. J. Ren- shaw, and Mr. Joseph Ditchtield. Much sympathy is expressed to the widow and family in their sad bereavement.

FLETCHER, RUSSELL, AND CO.'S FOOTBALL TEAM. On Saturday the above team and their friends to the number of about 40 met together at the Red Lion, Stockton, Heath to celebrate their vic- tory in the recent Works Competition. In the afternoon a bowling match was held, Married v. Single,the benedicts just winning. A capital knife and fork tea was provided, to which justice was done. After the tables had been cleared a capital programme was provided,Mr. W.Waterfield officiat- ing at the piano. The following is the programme: —Pianoforte solo, Mr. Watertield ; songs (2), Mr. E. Sutcliffe ; songs (2), Mr. W. Woodcock ; song, Mr.J. Evans ; songs (2),Mr. F. Heesom ; song, Mr. Peters; song, Mr. White; clarionet solos (2), Mr. Adcroft. During an interval in the programme Mr. J.Lawless proposed a toast, viz., the health of the Fletcher, Russell, and Co's football team. lie said it was a pleasing duty for him to perform. In the compe- tition the team played 4 matches and scored 77 points against a dropped goal (4) point3,never hav- ing their lines crossed. He thought this spoke well for the abilities of the team. If there was any little fault in their play it only needed mentioning to have the matter rectified. This had been a pleas- ing feature, and had given great satisfaction to the team committee. Their opponents thought they were perfect and faultless,but the "good old cherry and white" soon dispelled this idea. His toast was "Health to Fietcher, Russell, and Co's football team." He hoped they mikht live long to wear the gold medals which they had won fairly and honestly against all opposition both good,bad and indifferent. Tile toast was enthusias- tically honoured. Mr. John W. Mills, in reply to the toasts, said the duty of replying to the toast should really fall on Mr. Heesom, the captain of the team, but as he was more at home on the football field than in making a speech the duty devolved upon him as secretary to the team. He said the team throughout the competition had endeavoured to play a good sound game of. football, and they thought they might at least congratulate them- selves that they had not tarnished the name they bore by their exhibitions of football.tTo Mr. Ernest Fletcher in particular, he gave the thanks of the team for his presence at the final tie, as the interest he had taken had been of great encouragement to each member of the team. Their opponents in the final tie made sure of victory but they reminded him of a great watch dog, " Their bark was worse than their bite," and after they had taught them not to shout until they were out of the wood and had won the victory, the team's triumph was completed in finding Mr. Fletcher there to receive the medals on their behalf. They had not won the medals by boasting whac. they could do, but by each and every man in the team doing his share of work, and to that they owed the victory. In conclusion, he thanked them for the toast and assured them that the team had derived benefit from the en- couragement they had received. If occasion again demanded it, each and every mem- ber of the team, was again prepared to do his best to maintain the name they had won as footballers in the Warrington district.. A most pleasant evening was brought to a close after a vote of thanks on the proposition ot Mr. Peters to the host and hostess, Mr. and Mrs. Coprock, to which Mr. Coppock suitably responded. A vote ot thanks was also passed to Mr. Bowers for his services as chairman, and Mr. Bowers,in reply, said he hoped they would soon have another pleasant gathering like the one they had spent that evening. The company then dispersed in the usual manner by singing "Auld Lang Syne," and" God Save the Queen."
