THE LATEST THING IN BICYCLES.

" I should deeply regret," writes the cycling editor of "Land and Water," being the first to saund a note of alarm amongst the holders of any company stock: but it is more than probable that the next few weeks will see the advent of a bicycle that will entirely revolu- tioniz• the trade. I have had the privilege of riding the very first machine of the new style. Its inventors claim that at last the pneumatic tyre has been superseded,and really I must confess that I am greatly inclined to agree with this view. lam not at liberty to go thoroughly into details of construction of the new [machine yet, but I can say the wheels are shod with solid rubber tyres while the frame is pneumatically slung. There is also an improvement in the build of the head of the machine. which greatly facilitates steering, The application of ' the pneumatic principle in the new machine is extremely simple and ingenious, and noes credit to the skill of the adaptor. On Monday I rode the machine a'couple of spins round S. James's Park. The roads therein,:as we all knew, are not the best for cycling, yet I got over them in a way that surprised me. The resilience was perfect. and at the same time there was an entire ab- sence of vibration in the handle bar. I rode at all speeds, from dead slow to the highest rate I dared in the presence of the law ; I also ran down the rough cobble- stone waterways by the side of the road, yet the result was the same, ease and comfort, and no extra labour re- quired to attain it. Later in the day I had a long spin, and I have formed the opinion that there is a future,and a great one, for this new rival of the pneumatic tyre. I should like to point out just two advantages suggested by the new, machine—first, the absence of inflated tyres will minimise the risk of breaking down on the road ; second, the new machine, so I am told, will be turned out in first-class style at a more popular price than the first-class machines of the hour. I believe that £lO or £l2 complete will be its retail price. Intending investors had better remember Captain Cuttle's advice, Stand by,' and wait for further developments. I may add that the machine I rode, being the first one made, does not satisfy the ideas of the inventor—nor does it contain all the points enumerated in the amended specifications of patent. The gentleman who purposes introducing this cycle to the public informs roe that the inventor is hav- ing a number of perfect mac Lines made within the next few days."

===== 0002601_18960523_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
TEETOTAL OCTOGENARIANS.

As a practical demonstration of the hygienic results of teetotalism, the National Temperance League hit upon the idea of holding a meeting of octogenarian total ab- stainers, which gathering took place on Thuraday even- ing in St. Martin's Town Hall, London. Forty-one abstainers, including three women, occupied seats on the platform, the age of everyone of them being between eighty and ninety. The Secretary informed the meeting that he had received evidence of the existence of over 200 octogenarian teetotallers. Taking 152 of these, 16 were ninety to ninety-four years old, 31 wore eighty-five to eighty-nine, and 105 were between eighty and eighty-four. A great number of letters had been received, includidg one from Mr. T. Walmsley, of Preston, who wrote thNe his wife, three sisters, and brother were, Ilk himself, all over eighty, and had been original members of the Preston Temperance Society from the date of its formation in 1832. During the evening Mr. Livesey, of the same town, referred to the great temperance work done by his father,and exhibited the original " modera- tion " pledge framed at Preston 63 years ago. Another speaker was Mr. Isaac Sharp, an abstainer of 60 years' standing, and an admirable specimen (to use his own words) of a man who had reached three times thirty years. He was followed by the Rev. Newman Hall,wh-o completed Li, eightieth year on Friday. The chairman (Dr. F. R. Lees) referred to his aged supporters on the platform as old men really young, with faculties unim- paired, who had exploded every alcoholic superstition. Mr. Henry Brown, of Dublin, as representinc, Ireland, claimed that the sister isle formulated the first total abstinence pledge, in 1836, in Dublin and Belfast.

Charles Baker, aged 43, was sentenced at the Old Bailey on Wednesday to penal servitude for seven years for bigamy. Ho had defrauded a number of well-to-do but extremely credulous women. Although Baker had been repeatedly in gaol, he appears to have been able to obtain admission to good society in his intervals of free- dom, and to find ready acceptance of his representation of himself as " a gentleman,"a " large Australian sheep- farmer," and " the son of a bishop."

===== 0002601_18960523_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESENTATION TO A L. & N.W. TtAIL WAY OFFICIAL AT EA.RLESTOWN.

On Saturday evening about 60 of the employee and efficials of the wagon department of the London and North Western Railway Company, at Eirlestown, met at the Viaduct hotel for the purpose of presenting to Mr. George Dale, a foreman at the works, on the occasion of his retirement on a pension, with tokens of their appre- ciation of his long connection with the works. The pro- ceedings commenced with an excellent knife and fork tea,which was served in capital style by Mr. A. Tunstall and thoroughly enjoyed. After the cloth had been re- moved, Mr. F. Harwood, chief foreman at the works, presided, and Mr. W. Gilmour acted vice-chairman. After the usual loyal toasts had been proposed and duly honoured, the Chairman proceeded with the presenta- tion. He said they were met together to take part in a duty whic4 to him, and he was sure he might say to them all, was a pleasing one. But before he proceeded with the presentation, it would perhaps not be out of place if he first eavelthem a brief sketch of their friend's c onnection with the Viaduct Works. Mr. Dale was a Staff rdshire man, and he told him (the speaker) that he Ferved his apprenticeship in the town of Stafford. Soon after he was out of his time he thought it wise to visit other shops so as to gain experience, and in the course of time he arrived at Eerlestown, and was eiven employ- ment at the Wagon Works, in the year 1856, just about 30 years ago. He continued at the works up to the spring of 1869 when he left to do the joiner work at the new houses in Williamson Square. He also worked a short time for the contractor who was building the new ' shops in the works, and commenced again for the com- pany in November 1870, and was on new work for about 18 monthe,when he was selected to take foreman Morris's duty, during the latter's sickness. About that time Mr. F. Williamson, whom some of them might remember was foreman in the saw-mill, left to fill an appointment in America, Mr. Norris was appointed foreman in the saw mill, and Mr. Dale was appointed foreman over the repairs in place of Mr. Norris, a position which he held for about 17 years, and for the past seven years or more he had been as they were aware, assistant foreman over the new work. This whole servitude with the London and North Western Railway Company which was from 38 to 39 years, or, to be strictly accur- ate, 38 years and four months, as he believed he was away one year and eight months. (Applause.) It must be very gratifying to their friend to know that during the whole time he had been on good terms with his fellow workmen, and was respected by all with whom he came in contact. He had been a good servant to the Company, being impartial and just to the men under his charge. He (Mr. Harwood) could look back and remember a series of foremen, and though be did not wish there to make any comparison, he might be permitted to say that none had exceeded in their desire and earnestness to worthily fill that position their excellent friend Mr. Dale. (Applause.) He then on be- half of the subscribers formally presented Mr. Dale with a handsome gold albert and walking stick, and asked bine to accept them as a token of the esteem and respect in whieh he was held by them, and he added that they all united in expressing the hope that Mr Dale might long be spared to enjey his well earned rest in health and happiness. (Applause.) Mr. Dale thanked hia fellow workmen for the testi- monial, and he also thanked Mr. Harwood for present- ing it to him. He could assure them that he felt it very much to have to break away from old friends. (Hear, hear.) The toast of " rite London and North-Western Rail- way Company "was proposed by Mr. Limond, and res- ponded to by Mr. W. Gilmour. Other toasts followed, and the evening was spent in a convivial manner, songs being given by Messrs. J. Lewis, W. Kay, J. Huddles- ton, J. Gibbons, W. Mawdsley, and J. Edgar, Mr. E. Leyland presiding at the piano. A vote of thanks to the Chairman on the motion of Mr. J. Swann, terminated a very pleasant evening.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

As a spectacular display the May Queen festival was a decided success, but I think the authorities— I mean the town authorities—might have had a little more consideration for the children. The day was hot and dusty, and the sufferings of the youngsters as they trudged along amidst the crowds of spectators and through the volumes of dust, can be better imagined than described. All this might have been remedied had the watering cart been dispatched over the line of route, say an hour, or even less, before the time the procession got under weigh. Near the place of assembly the dust was frightful, and before the children could get on to the hard roads they were literally covered with it. The cost of watering this particular por- tion ot the route would not have been very heavy but the benefit would have been incalculable.

I have on previous occasions drawn attention to the want of a mortuary for the reception of stran- gers who happen to come by their death suddenly in Earlestown. Instances have arisen on several occasions proving the necessity of such a build- ing being erected, and this week the provis- ion of a proper building has again been brought in evidence. An old gentleman died sud- denly at the railway station, and his body was taken by the police to a public-house. The landlord, however, refused to take it in, alleg- ing that he had no accommodation. The police had then to convey the body further up the town to the Ram'.4 Head.where luckily they found a rest- ing place. Supposing, however, all the publicaus had taken the same course and refused to take in the body the police would have been in a fix.l think the matter should be taken up by the District Council at once, and some provision made to pre- vent bodies being carted about the streets in this manner. This is not the first case of the kind.

The number of cyclists in Earlestown has during the last few weeks increased to an extraordinary extent,and yet they are without the social influence of a club. lam an advocate of social and friendly intercourse, and there is nothing which will tend to pcoruote this better than a club including both sexes What I should like to see is a strong tour- ing club of a respectable character which would advocate Saturday afternoon runs during the cy- cling season.

Another fad of mine is a cyclists' lantern parade in aid of some local charity. The affair need not he of an expensive character, but I feel certain that if properly worked out it would attract equally as much attention as the May Queen festival. The Harriers' could be asked to assist as collectors,and I fancy after the payment ot !?xpenses a respectable sum could be handed over,

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

THURSDAY.

In the House of Commons on Thursday Colonel Rus- SELL asked Mr. Chamberlain if he had received any con- firmation of the sentence passed upon the leaders of the reform movement at Pretoria. Mr. Chamberlain re- ferred the hon. member to the telegrams published in the papers. With regard to the treatment of the pri- soners in gaol, he said he had not seen any suggestion that the insanity and suicide of one of the prisoners was due to the severity and hardships of imprisonment. He had received a telegram from the British Agent at Pre- toria stating that the President of the South African Re- public had promised that special gaol rules and regula- tions w‘ould be immediately framed, and also that the housing and accommodation would be vastly improved. The final sentences upon the four men who had been condemned to death would be considered next week.

Asked by Mr. KILBRIDE why the importation of Irish store pigs into Lancashire had been prohibited, Mr. LONG said the regulations were imposed by the local authority in order to prevent the introduction of swine fever into the county, and it rested with the local authority to say when those regulations might be with- drawn.

Asked by Sir C. DILICE what he intended to do with reference to the petition from nine members of the jury who tried William Kearsley for the murder of Detective Kidd at Wigan (in which the jurymen stated that had they known the full facts of the case before .they gave their verdict they would have found Kearsley not guilty), Sir M. White RIDLEY said that after carefully consiier- ing all the circumstances of the case, and after consult- ing the judge who tried the prisoner, he had come to the conclusion that Kearsley was rightly convicted ; and al- though there were circumstances which justified him in advising the exercise of the prerogative of mercy, he saw no grounds for reconsidering the case. Mr. Balfour proposed the suspension of ths twelve o'clock rule, so that the debate on the Agricultural Rat- ing Bill might proceed after midnight. The motion was carried by 215 votes to 103. On clause 2, which pro- vides for the payment out of the local taxation account in respect of the deficiency arising from the exemption granted under the previous clause, Mr. Lloyd-George moved a drafting amendment to provide that the amount to be paid to the local authorities should be described as paid to make up the " differ- ence " and not the " deficiency " in the rates. After some debate, the Solicitor General promised that the points raised should receive careful consideration be- fore the report stage of the bill, and Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE thereupon withdrew his amendment. Mr. CHAPLIN next proposed that the payment out of the Local Taxation Account in respect of the deficiency should be " during the continuance of this Act," and the motion was carried by 223 votes to 102. Several amendments w-,re then dealt with and rejected with the aid of the closure.

aln the House of Lords the Reserve Forces Bill passPd through Committee. Lord LANSDOWNE, Secretary for War, said we have at present a reserve force of 80,000 men, who can only be recalled to the colours in case of imminent danger and national emergency. The bill gives the Government power to recall the reservists in their first year "when warlike expeditions are in con- templation or in progress." The House will meet again on June 8.

WARRINGTON RUGBY FOOTBALL CLUB.

ANNUAL MEETING.

On Thursday evening there was a strong muster of member of the above club at the Workingmen's Club room, in the Market Place, when the annual meeting was held. Councillor Davies presided and was supported on the platform by Mr. J. E. Warren (secretary), Mr. S. W. Jeffery (assistant secretary), and Mr. T. Pemberton (treasurer).

===== 0002601_18960523_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS TO THE SECRETARY.

Mr. DAVIES said he thought it would be out of place in him if he allowed that opportunity to pass without, in the name of the club, thanking their worthy secretary for his efforts in the interests of Rugby football. (Hear, hear and applause.) Mr. Warren not only deserved their congregations but he (Mr. Davies) was sure he deserved and had the congratulations of every Rugby football club in Lancashire. It was common talk in ootball circles, what wonderful work he had done and how indefatigable he had been. They themselves knew how determined he was with respect to that very vexed question of broken time. (Hear, hedr.) Mr. Warren, backed by a number of other gentlemen, thought that players of Rugby football were not being treated properly, and that there ought to be some con- sideration made for workingmen who were obliged to give up some of their time for football, in the shape of payment for the loss of such time. (Applause.) There was very strong feeling about it, and he would remind them of the very important work their secretary had done not only for his own club but for all the clubs in ' Lancashire. He had carried the whole thing through independent of all the antagonism which was opposing him. Mr. Warren would not be forgotten by those workingmen whose interests he had been furthering and he and others thought a workingman had the right to play just as much as a rich man. There were many other matters besides broken time, but be (Mr. Davies) thought thdt so far as the Northern Union was concetned it bad been a matter of pleasure to them to see bow very harmoniously things had worked. Those independent men of Swinton, Salford, and ether clubs who refused to have anything to do with the Northern Union but would stick to the County Club, had now cast in their lot with them. They had only needed to be left a little timeito be taught that the proper way of dealing with workingmen players was to adopt the principles of the Northern Union. (Applause.) He proposed that they tender to Mr. Warren their heartiest congratulations and thanks for what he had done during the past year. (Applause.) Councillor Tiartnorr said he had great pleasure indeed in seconding the proposition. Mr. Warren had given a lot of time in furthering the interests of the Union and the least they could do was to show that they thoroughly appreciated the efficient work he had done for the War- rington Club and Rugby football generally. (Ap- plause.) The vote was then passed with acclamation. THE REPORT.

Councillor TINNION proposed that the Secretary and Treasurer's reports should be adopted. He was sure they would all of them look back upon last year's work- ing of the club with great satisfaction. Speaking of the matter dealt with in the secretary's report, he said the football bad been of a high class nature, and had been viewed with great pleasure by the spectators,and it was something to congratulate themselves upon that they stood so well as they did. With regard to the treasurer's report, that was even more satisfactory than the other. (Applause.) They had had the pleasure the whole of the season of witnessing good football and what was more they had a good balance in hand. (Hear, hear.) He:thought that spoke volumes for the way the club had been managed. (applause.) .The speaker did not think there was any club in the whole of the Union, compara- tively speaking, which had been so successful as the Warrington Club. (Applause.) Mr.GEoeoz SMITH seconded the motion,and remarked that at the dune sports last year the prizes were marked "made in Germany." That was not right,and he meant to ask the incoming committee to go against that sort of thing, as Englishmen. (Hear, hear.) They were spending the public money.and the public had a right to speak on that subject. He hoped the committee would see to it in the future. The CHAIRMAN said he was asked to inform Mr. Smith that he was wrong about Germany, as the goods were made in Vienna, and should have been marked Austria. (Laughter, and a voice: "That's worse.") ii,The reports were adopted..

CYCLE LEARNING IN THE STREETS

A CORONER'S WARNING.

At Chelsea on Thursday, an inquest on held on the re- mains of Eliza Houghton, widow of the late Captain William Boughton, who was knocked down by a bicyclist on the Thames Embankment a fortnight ago, and died from injuries and shock. The evidence showed that the bicylist was learning to ride.—The Coroner condemned the practice of learning in the public streets, and the jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death." A young naan who was riding a bicycle alosg West- minster Bridge-road, London, on Wednesday, was knocked down and killed. He was passing the end of Tower-street, when a horse and van turning out of the street knocked him down and ran over him. He was taken to the hospital, but was dead when he arrived. His machine was smashed to pieces.

As the result of the inquest on the sixty-two victims of the Micklefield Colliery disaster, the jury on Wednes- day found that the explosion was caused by gas, was purely accidental, and that no blame attached to any- one.

_ A storm of a cyclonic character, accompanied by a deluge of rain, broke over the golf links at Sandwich on Wednesday while the second day's play for the amateur championship was in progress. The marquees and their contents were completely wrecked. William Seaman, charged with the murder of John G. Levy and Anna Gale at Mile End, was convicted and sentenced to death at the Old Bailey on Monday. The prisoner,it will be remembered, sprang into the street from the roof of the house in which he had killed Levy and his housekeeper. He offered no defence.

A BOGYKFOR MAR-B,IED WOMEN The information contained im;this book cught to be known by:every married woman, and it will not, harm the unmarried to read. The book is conveniently divided. Into 12 chapters. The first chapter treats o! the changes at puberty,or when a girl becomes a woman- The second chapter.taeats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint; points out the best age for marriage, and, who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful in- formation, that one can ordinarily get only from an in, telligent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage.. of bloed; relations; and condemns such marriages as a rule. Chapter four treats of,Ohe signs of. pregnancy. The fifth chapter tells how a Iceman should live during the pregnant state. The sixtk chapter treats of mishaps and how to. avoid them. The seventh chapter treats cf maternal impressions arrd shows that birthmarks are not due tolongings on the -part of the mother, bat rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how• to have easy confinements. EVERY PERSON'S FUTURE. Every person's future is, to a certain extent, in his own hand. Many a future which might have been bril- liant has been darkened by lack of the spirit of self-help. Many a home might have been gladdened by life and health and comfort, has been saddened by poverty, illness and death, in many cases wholly preventible. The spring and summer months are, from a sanitary point of view, among the most important of the year. Due care may now mean a healthy and happy summer and autumn, and robust constitution to meet the trials of next winter. Neglect may now mean a joyless sum- mer, a trying autumn, and a broken constitution to succumb uniler the attacks of any exceptional trial. There are indeed few that do not feel the system needs recruiting. With some it is " only a little cough," "tendency to bronchitis," and others, " not quite up to the mark," "a sort of all over sinking feeling," " too weak to do anything about the house," "these recurring , headaches,"" no appetite for his food," &c. These and a dozen others are common expressions which all point , to the urgent need of some good tonic. What change of air can do for you in a few months' time—if you are no I worse then than you are now—a course of some good tonic mixture, a reliable medicine of established reputa- tion and of proved virtue will do now. There are several tonic mixtures to be had, but none which have been so uniformly successful as Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, the Vegetable Tonic. It is the Best REMEDY of the age for WEAKNESS, NERVOUSNESS, LOW SPIRITS, MELANCHOLY, CHEST AFFECTION, LOSS OF APPETITE, INDIGESTION, AND BLOOD DISORDERS. • Avoid Imitations. See the name" Gwilym Evans" on the Label, Stamp and Bottle. Prices :—Bottles, 2s. 9d.; double size, 4.5. 6d. To be had of all Chemists, or direct from the Proprietors : QUININE BITTERS MANUFACTURING Co LTD., LLANELLY, SOUTH WALES.

ALLEGED DESERVION.—CharIes Johnson, of Piror-street was again charged with deserting his wife and family. It will be remembered that the case was adjourned for a month to prove means, but this his not yet been done as defendant is ill anti has not the means to contribute to his wife's main- tainaace. Mr. F. A. Lake appeared for the defendant. The case was now adjourned another two months. 1 EARLESTOWN V. LIVERPOOL.—An eleven of Liverpool I met Earlestown on the borne ground on Saturday after- noon, and although on previous occasions Earlestown have managed to obtain the victory, in this instance they had to suffer defeat. Liverpool went first to the wickets and succeeded in compiling 100 in a little over an hour, Melly batting very carefully for his 21. Hus- band and Dennis opened the innings for EarleAown,the latter after adding 2, being cleverelv caught by Ring- rose. Miller and Barker made a good stand, but the latter was unfortunate in being struck several times on the hand with the ball. Bell was also placed at a dis- advantage by falling and twisting his knee. RUNCORN FREE LIBRARY COMMITTEE MEETING. The Free Library Committee met in the Runcorn Town Hall on Tuesday evening. Present : Mr. Elandley (in the chair), Messrs. T. Riley, J. Pritchard, W. J. Cunuingham and the Rev. J. Simpson. The cash statement for the month showed payments amounting to £9 163. 93., of which £4 161. 91. was due April 21st. The remaining item of £5 was put down as salaries. The receipts were by tines lie. 101., tickets ss. 81., catalogues 41., papers 23. 3d., subscriptions is., balance due £8 159. Si. Dotal £9 163. 91. The report of the Book Selection Committee was sub- mitted, together with a list of nooks totalling 228 volumes amounting to £26 173. 31. The Committee ex- pressed their approval of the report, and it was decided to recommend the Council to purchase the hooks on the list presented by the Committee. It was also decided to ask for the transfer of £5O to the Library Fund. Ac- counts amounting te £5 15s. 2d. were passed for pay- ment. The donations included the following :-4 water colour paintings by Mr. W. H. Leathwood, a native of the town, to be hung in the r-ading room, by Mr. S. Taylor, J.P.. C.C. ; Rt-fiections on the festhetic principle," with tales and poems by Mr. W. H. Leath- wood ; Great Yarwood and Liverpo3l. by Mr. J. Simp- son, of Runcorn, Frodsham and flelsby (illustrated)Mr. S Bellamy, a copy of same for reading room, J. J' Wheelan.

On Tuesday evening, at the Velodrome de la Seine, Paris, Tom Linton established new cycling records from the fifth mile, covering 30 miles within the hour. On Tuesday afternoon the body of a child was found in the nightsoil cart at the Holt Town Depot. The di- visional police surgeon (Dr. Heslop) examined it, and discovered that a piece of cotton wool had been rammed down the child's throat, causing suffocation. At the in- quest on Wednesday,the Coroner remarked that the case had all the appearances of child murder, and he ad- journed the inquest until the 3rd proximo.
